ring root;

Then arise from helpless moping nor repine at each annoy;
There is room for wider hoping if your days are void of joy.
Lift your eyes to yon Day-Giver, look up higher hoping still;
Though the rain is on the river yet the sun is on the hill.

SUNLIGHT
ON THE HILLS

I
COMING back to her dressing-room Kay Douglas
could just hear the orchestra playing the final bars of the
National Anthem. Above its familiar notes the faint rustle
and whisper of a dispersing audience. They sounded, she
thought, humming the tune herself under her breath, like
a lot of fidgety mice in their anxiety, now that the play was
over, to get outside. It had been a good house too. Better
still, a "nice" house. Appreciative and understanding
and immensely sensitive. Isabel Marsden, meeting Kay
in the wings ten seconds before her first entrance, had
said: "It's nice tonight," with an artist's quick intuition
of sympathy "out front," and a moment later Kay had
sensed it too. Her entrance had been greeted with a storm
of applause the more remarkable because the show wasn't
even new. It had been running for nearly three months, so
that the applause, spontaneous, deafening, tremendously
inspiring, was doubly dear. It had, in fact, stopped the
show for an appreciable moment and across the expanse of
stage carpet that separated them Tony Seabright, who
played "opposite" her, grinned at her. Kay had found
herself thinking in that moment of time and even while
she smiled her acceptance of the greeting:
"He's a nice boy. Really nice. Beau Alfriston would've
hated this. Been jealous. But Tony, he's nice." Then the
storm had died away, the show had wound its triumphant
way through three tensely gripping acts, and at the end
9
10
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
there'd been eight curtains. The audience had refused to
be satisfied. The pit and the gallery had actually risen in
their places and roared their approval, and even the stalls
had been shaken a little in the rut of their customary bore-
dom. So the curtain had gone up first on the entire cast,
then on the four principal characters, then on Tony and
Kay, hand in hand, smiling at the audience and at each
other, then again and again and again, because the play
was so obviously "hers," on Kay alone. Until at last the
leader of the orchestra had tapped his music rack and
stopped the noise by the simple expedient of plunging
headlong and very noisily into the National Anthem.
In the wings Tony Seabright was waiting for her.
"What're you doing over the week-end, Kay?"
"Lots of things," Kay said. Black shadows under her
eyes and a pallor even make-up couldn't altogether disguise
gave the lie to the gay inconsequence of her voice. "Here,
there, and everywhere, uphill, downhill" She broke
off sharply, caught her breath on a sigh that gayety
couldn't stifle. "Gosh, Tony, I'm tired."
Tony said, considering her :
"You're looking tired too. Anything wrong, Kay?"
"No, just tiredness," Kay said. She caught a glimpse
of herself in a mirror hanging against a white-washed
wall, thought swiftly, a little dismayed at the reflection:
"He's right. I look a hag. I've got to slow up. To take
things easy unless I want to break altogether. If Tony
thinks I look tired I must be ghastly." She went on to her
dressing-room, smiling at the boy as she went. "Don't
fuss, there's a darling, and in any case it's not very polite
of you to tell me I'm looking a wreck, Tony."
"You know I didn't mean that," Tony said. He fol-
lowed her, protesting. He was young, two years her junior,
and terribly in love with her. "You're marvelous. You're
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
II
always marvelous. Only if you're tired-let me take
you home, Kay. We'll have supper sent in-let's have a
quiet evening."
"You're sweet," Kay said. With her hand on the knob
of her dressing-room door she stopped, dismissing him.
"You're the sweetest thing I know, Tony, and I'd love to
go home for a tête-à-tête supper with you if I weren't
already booked up three deep."
"It's Veryan, isn't it?" Tony said. His dark eyes were
tragic with longing. “Isn't it, Kay?"
"No," Kay said. "It's every one. Everything. Every-
body. All this. The lights and the flowers. Being popular
and run after. Having lots of men in love with me. Big
men, Tony, with old names. And money. All the money
they need. You've never been poor, Tony. You've always
had a nice home and most of the things you wanted, while
I-" she broke off for the second time. "If you knew
what my life used to be like you'd understand. Why I've
got to grab all this while I can with both hands before it
slips where I can't get it. Why I can't ever see enough of
any of it. There'll be time enough to sit at home when I'm
old. When men don't want me any longer. When I'm not
a star. That'll be all I'll be good for, but now, don't grudge
me a few good times, Tony."
"I don't grudge you anything," Tony Seabright said.
"It's only you're all in and you know it. You can't keep
it up. I'm dippy about you, but that's neither here nor
there. What does matter is you, and the way you're going
you'll crack up. How many hours' sleep did you get in last
night? Or the night before? Or the night before that?
Two? Three?"
"About that I expect," Kay said. She pushed her hair
back from her eyes with a gesture that held, had she only
realized it, all the weariness in the world. "Don't croak,
12
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
Tony. You're a lamb and I adore you, but I've got to go.
Dick Veryan loathes being kept waiting. You're sweet, but
you mustn't fuss about me. I'm perfectly all right you
know. Even if I am tired. 'Night, darling."
There was a note waiting for her in her dressing-room.
It lay on the top of a box that held crimson carnations
and her dresser jerked her head in its direction.
"From his lordship, same as usual. My, but you look
tired, Miss."
"I'm dead," Kay said. She dropped into a chair but her
eyes glowed at sight of the carnations. She lifted them up,
sniffing them greedily, read the note that, written in a
heavy sprawling hand, bore Richard Veryan's signature.
"How do you do it?" the note said. "You look lovelier
tonight than I've ever seen you. I'll call for you sharp at
11.15."
She said, tearing up the note and laughing:
"Love's blind, you know, Mattie. I look a hag and any
one with half an eye can see it. Even Mr. Seabright noticed
it, and Lord Richard says I look lovelier than he's ever
seen me."
"You'd ought to go easy," Mattie said. She was unfas-
tening, with swift, deft fingers, the hooks of the dress
Kay had worn in the last act. "Is lordship's batty about
you, an' it's only natural 'e shouldn't see what ordin'ry
folks sees. But you're all in and anybody with sense knows
it. If you want to be told what to do by somebody as 'as
lived a lot longer nor you, you'll go home to bed an' you'll
stay in bed till time to start for the theater Monday. That's
if you've got any sense." She added darkly and grimly:
"Which seein' the way you're goin' on I have me doubts
about."
"You're an old scaremonger, Mattie. Can you see me
spending the week-end in bed? I'm going out for the day
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
13
with Lord Richard. I'd get up out of my grave rather than
miss tomorrow."
"That's what you'll be doin', too, 'fore much longer,
the way you're goin' now," Mattie said. She crossed the
little room, hung up in a cupboard the cloth of silver
sheath that had caused such a sensation in dressmaking
circles, took down a black chiffon dance frock, stood with
it over her arm while, with fingers as sure and deft as her
own, Kay took off her stage make-up, replaced it with the
rouge and powder and lipstick of everyday normal life,
and then slid it over Kay's shining red head. "Anybody'd
think," she said, grumbling, "you'd have a bit o' sense
seein' what that doctor's told you a'ready."
Kay said quickly, almost fearfully:
"Don't. I don't want to be reminded of what he said.
Besides, he was only trying to scare me. I'm perfectly fit.
It's only that I'm tired. Give me my coat, Mattie, it's a
quarter-past”
The knock on the door was synonymous with her voice.
She called gayly, lightly: "Come in!" turned with out-
stretched hands to meet the man who opened the door in
answer to her welcome. "I'm ready, Richard."
"You're adorable," Richard Veryan said. He crossed
the room with a single swift stride, kissed without mak-
ing the action appear either theatrical or ludicrous, the
hands she gave him. "I thought the damned show'd never
end. Give me Miss Douglas's flowers and her bag, Mattie,
I'll take care of them."
"Better take care o' her instead," Mattie said darkly
and stood by the door watching them as they went out.
"She'll need a bit o' care seems to me, the way she's
goin'."
There was a little crowd waiting at the stage door.
Stenographers, shop girls, boys with hungry eyes, men
14
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
whose lips gave away their character. Voices, shrill, cock-
ney, envious, overtopped even the roar of passing traffic.
"That's her. My, I wouldn't mind her luck.”
"Bit of orlright, ain't she?"
"Oo's the boy friend? Thinks 'e's somebody, don't 'e?"
"An' why shouldn't 'e? Takin' out a bit o' fluff like 'er."
Kay was accustomed to it now. Two years ago it had
all been a marvelous novelty. Something to look forward
to at the close of every evening's performance. She hadn't
been a popular star then. Just Miss Kay Douglas who
played small parts or understudied; who watched, with
eyes only one degree less envious than those of the typists
and shop girls who made up the crowd at the stage door,
the triumphant progress of actresses who had made good.
Who played big parts in first-class productions; who wore
beautiful clothes even in the street; who, shrouded in furs
and decked with jewels that glittered and sparkled in the
light of the lamp over the doorkeeper's office, went out
laughing to supper with exquisite young men whose shirt
studs were real pearls and whose cars, chauffeur-driven,
luxurious, coroneted, stood at the curb waiting with open
doors to welcome their radiant burden. Kay Douglas, two
years ago, had watched it all, at the end of a five-year
struggle depressed and a little disheartened. One got so
tired of walking on. Of understudying a girl who'd drag
herself even half-dead on to the stage rather than let her
understudy have a chance of trying on her shoes and find-
ing them a comfortable fit. Twice, she'd understudied
quite decent parts too, and she'd worked. She could look
back now and laugh, remembering how she'd worked at
those parts and how desperately she'd prayed for a chance.
For a broken ankle, a shot of laryngitis, 'flu that turned
into pneumonia, not dangerous pneumonia but bad enough,
for anything at all that would keep Cora Marsland in bed
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
15
for just one night. Even for one solitary matinée that
might have given her understudy a chance to prove her
worth. And the chance had never come. Cora Marsland
watched her step far too warily to put chances in the way
of ambitious little understudies, and then quite suddenly,
miraculously, Bryan Onslow, lunging bad-temperedly out
of Dickson's office just over two years ago, had seen her
and stopped her.
"Now," he'd said, and with a hand under her chin had
tipped her face up to the light, "where the hell have you
been? What the devil" He'd turned back into Dick-
son's room, pulling her after him. "You're a fool, Dickie.
Here's Rosemary sitting on your own doorstep and you
haven't the sense to recognize her when you meet her."
Then had followed hours and hours of the hardest work
Kay had ever known. Dickson, caught napping, had made
up his mind to get even with some one somewhere and
he'd taken out his ill-temper on her. He'd switched from
abuse to sarcasm, from sarcasm to curses, without trou-
bling to pick or choose his words, and again and again
Kay, desperate and struggling, had asked herself, too
utterly weary even to argue, whether the game could pos-
sibly be worth the candle. Then Bryan Onslow had inter-
fered once again.
"Let her alone, Dickie," he'd said. He'd crossed the
stage to where, sick with weariness and fear and humilia-
tion, Kay stood in a corner. "Now then, you've got your
own idea of Rosemary, haven't you? You think Dickie's
all wrong, don't you? And that he hasn't got her by
streets. That's what you think, isn't it?"
"Yes," Kay said. She thought, "I'll be fired for this.
Of course I'll be fired," but she'd stuck nevertheless to her
guns. "I don't think he understands”
"O.K.," Bryan Onslow said coolly. He winked at Dick-
16
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
son, cursing under his breath in the empty sheathed stalls.
"We'll stand by and see whether you understand her. For-
get all Dickie's told you, and fire ahead." He added
sharply, because she didn't attempt to move and already it
was nearly three o'clock and he'd had no lunch, "Get on
with it, my good girl, nobody's going to eat you."
Somehow, after that, Kay had "got on with it." She'd
stumbled at first badly, but little by little she'd got a hold
on herself. Dickson, cursing in the stalls, had continued
to curse, but Bryan Onslow had been oddly quiet. Con-
centrating on the stage and on the badly-dressed, red-
headed girl who in some way explicable only to herself
had seen into his mind and out of it had dragged the Rose-
mary he'd created there. And at the end he'd laughed.
"Now," he'd said to Dickson, "d'you see what a fool
you are? My God, man, she's the girl. She isn't pretending
to be Rosemary. She is Rosemary. Now go back to the
beginning of the last act and we'll have it through again,
and forget Dickson, see? Forget him as if he'd never
existed. He's only a blind idiot anyway. Now Stephen's
over there, you're here. Now.”
The play had only run six weeks, but at the end of it
Leslie Dashwood had engaged her for Dorothy Rawsden
in Golden Butterflies at thirty pounds a week. And then,
when that too closed, Simon Arthurs had sent for her and
signed her up to play Brenda in A Lady of Leisure at a
salary of fifty a week. She'd been playing Brenda now for
over three months, and her name was up in lights over
the theater along with Tony Seabright. She'd been para-
graphed, photographed, sketched, pointed at; she'd heard
people say of her enviously, "That's Kay Douglas, the
actress"; she'd seen her picture in the Tatler and the
Sketch, side by side with glittering stars of the Social
world and, less exaltedly, in the center page of Sunday
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
17
picture papers. She'd exchanged the unspeakable bed-sit-
ting-room in the Fullham Road with its smells of cheap
linoleum and babies' washing and cabbage water for a tiny
service flat off Portman Square; she was waited for, as
Cora Marsland had once been waited for, by hungry-eyed
boys and girls at the stage door, and for ever and always,
whenever there was time for them, there were parties.
And men. All sorts and conditions of men but all of them
with money they wanted to spend on making her happy
and giving her a good time. All of them ready at a mo-
ment's notice to run in reply to her summons, to wait at
her beck and call, to do her bidding, to make life the most
marvelous adventure.
Leaning back in the warmth of Richard Veryan's big
car Kay thought:
"It's good to be alive. It's marvelous to be young and
pretty and popular. To be wanted like this. Just suppose
one were dead and never knew" Then she shivered
suddenly and Richard Veryan pulled up the window.
"You're not cold, are you? Here, darling, pull the rug
farther up." He leant over her, fussing, solicitous for her
comfort, charming. "This damned traffic holds us up so
long. Are you cold, Kay?"
"No," Kay said, and forced her limbs to keep still. "It's
only Oh, Richard, let's be terribly gay tonight. Let's
dance every dance and forget all the things we don't want
to remember. I want to go on till I drop. Till I can't keep
my eyes open another minute. So that when I get home
I'll have to sleep instead of lying awake thinking." She
laughed sharply, without any mirth, so that for a second
he looked at her queerly. Then she slid a hand through his
arm and when she spoke again her voice had lost its queer
driving note of urgency. "I'm talking frightful rubbish,
but let's be terribly gay tonight just the same, shall we?"
II
LEANING back in his chair Sir Godfrey Horton said
slowly:
"I gather you haven't been acting on the advice I gave
you three months ago. To rest and sleep and live in the
open air. That is the case, isn't it?"
"How can I sleep and live in the open air?" Kay said.
Her voice was edgy and frightened. "My job you know
what my job is, Doctor. Girls like me we can't just slide
out of things and live in the country because we get colds
one on top of another. We've got to live. How d'you sup-
pose we're going to live or sleep or eat or do anything if
we don't work? And in the country? Don't you see there's
no sense in telling me to go and live in the country? You
might as well tell me to go and live in the middle of the
Atlantic Ocean."
"I realize all that," Sir Godfrey said evenly. He changed
his position in the big mahogany chair, crossed his left leg
in its immaculate striped trousering over his right, made
a tent of his fingers and considered her through eyes that
were steely gray and utterly impersonal. Kay thought,
watching him: "I'm not a person to him at all. Just a case.
It doesn't mean a thing to him-" "I realize all that. I
realize it perfectly. But as a doctor I am obliged to tell you
the truth. You will admit that at least. And in your case
you are young. Given care and the treatment I have pre-
scribed there is every chance of your complete recovery.
18
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
19
As things are, with the life you lead" He shrugged
beautifully-clad shoulders and the shrug covered far more
than he would have liked to put into words. "You must
believe me when I tell you that the advice I give you is
the only thing possible if you are ever to be a tolerably
healthy woman, Miss Douglas. You will understand I am
sure that with your past history"
"But in these days," Kay said desperately, "people don't
believe in heredity, Doctor. You know they don't. It's just
used as a bugbear. A bogey to frighten people. Even you
yourself-only a minute ago"
"Exactly," Sir Godfrey said patiently. He thought, with
a mind by no means as impersonal as his eyes: "The
whole thing is a damned shame. And the girl's frightened
to death. Scared stiff. If I dared I'd let her down more
lightly and I can't. She's got to know the truth, to face
facts now, before it's too late." He put his hands against
the edge of his desk, leant forward over them. "I said, if
you remember, that I didn't believe in heredity as it used
to be believed in by our grandfathers who, if a man had
a tubercular parent, assumed, without taking the slightest
further trouble in the matter, that he would be tubercular
himself, and that, of course, is a theory long since ex-
ploded. But, and there is a big but, Miss Douglas, a man
with a history of tuberculosis behind him has a predisposi-
tion to the disease if you understand what I mean. It isn't,
of necessity, born in him, as years ago people believed,
but the tendency to the disease is there and it is against
that tendency we have to fight. A girl with no tubercular
history may get colds one on top of another and throw
them off with no permanent result. A girl, on the contrary,
with such a history may find herself landed with the dis-
ease itself, and in your case"
"I've got the history, haven't I?" Kay said. She smiled
20
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
at him but her lips were dry. "Have I got the disease too,
Doctor?"
Just for a second the only sounds breaking the silence of
the big sunny consulting-room were the ticking of the
clock on the desk at Sir Godfrey's elbow and, from far
away outside its thick walls, the dull rumble of buses
punctuated now and then by the impatient horns of cars
and taxis. Then Sir Godfrey picked up a couple of X-ray
photographs that lay to the right of his blotter and slid
them into the drawer.
"Yes," he said. "It's better to be honest, isn't it, Miss
Douglas? At the moment the damage is so slight as to be
almost negligible, but things of this kind have an unpleas-
ant habit of refusing to remain negligible. If you will take
my advice now"-he paused a moment so that the tiny
adverb took on a special significance of its own "there
is no reason why, in a couple of years"
"Yes," Kay said. "I know. Drink milk and eat eggs.
Go to bed at ten o'clock. No late nights or hot rooms.
Fresh air. Country food, the open air. Live in it, eat in it,
sleep in it. I know all that, Doctor, but it's out of the ques-
tion. If I've got consumption it'll have to kill me, that's
all. Where d'you suppose I can get money to do the things
you want me to do? I work for my living, Doctor. I've
worked for it for the last seven years."
"There are hospitals to meet cases like yours," Sir God-
frey said, and Kay got up.
"Yes," she said. "I know. Where people live by rules
and regulations. Where you eat when you're told and not
when you're hungry, where you go to bed by the clock,
where you go for walks because you're told to go, rest
when you're told to rest. I know those places." She fum-
bled blindly in her purse, dragged out a five pound note,
laid it with two half-crowns on the desk beside her, and
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
21
smiled at him out of eyes that were drowned in tragedy.
"I'd sooner go on my own way, Doctor. At least when
I'm alive I'll be living, and the other way" She
laughed with terrible bitterness. "Be happy while you may,
you'll be a long time dead's my motto, and it's as good as
any, taking it all in all. Anyway it won't be your fault if
I die young. You've been terribly nice, but I think, just
the same, I'll go my own way and make the best of what's
left."
Back in the sitting-room of her own tiny flat, Mattie
brought her tea.
"You look fair done. What on earth've you bin doin'
with yourself, Miss?"
"I've been to the doctor," Kay said. She took off her
hat, flung it on a chair in the opposite corner of the room,
ran a hand through soft red curls that felt damp and
sticky. "He's been lecturing me, so don't you start, Mattie.
What's the matter with you all anyway? Oh for pity's
sake" Her voice cracked and broke on a note of edgy
irritation. "Can't you leave me alone without looking as
though you expected me to drop dead where I stand?"
She saw the shadow on the woman's face, caught herself
up with swift spontaneous compunction. "I'm sorry, Mat-
tie. Only I'm tired and fussed. And every one keeps on
telling me how thin I'm getting. Be a good soul and leave
me alone for a bit, will you? I'm all in, but I'll be as right
as rain when I've had tea."
Mattie said:
"Why don't you take your frock off and get a bit of a
lie-down? 'Tain't natural folks, even strong folks, can keep
on the run same as you do and not git done in sooner or
later. Now listen to me, dearie. You go and lay down for
a bit on your bed and I'll bring your tea in to you. You'll
22
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
feel a different girl if you get a bit of a nap before you go
to the theater."
"I don't want" Kay said, and then checked the
words almost as she spoke them. "All right, I'll go. It is
only that I'm tired. And doctors are such scaremongers.
You don't think I'm getting terribly thin, do you, Mattie?"
"Not as you'd notice it," Mattie lied. She thought:
"That doctor told her something that's scared her proper,
an' 'tain't no good frightenin' her any more." She followed
Kay into the little bedroom, fussing with pillows, with
Kay's dressing-gown, with blinds and windows. "Now
you lay right there, dearie, and if you goes to sleep so
much the better. You needn't be afraid I won't call you
in time for the show."
But lying there with the tea untasted on the tray by her
side Kay knew she'd never in her life felt less like sleep.
She thought, staring at the wall that faced her aching,
frightened eyes. "It's so unfair. So rottenly unfair. After
the way I've had to work. For this to happen, now, just
when I'm making good, it's so unfair."
Her thoughts, running round and round like squirrels
trapped in a cage, steadied themselves, shot backwards
over the years. To the beginning of life in the dingy Bal-
ham side street, and from there found their way along
the trail that widening and twisting led at last to Kay
Douglas, popular, sought after, earning seventy-five
pounds a week, in the luxurious little bedroom of her
luxurious little flat.
It was funny almost, to look back on all those years.
To see oneself then and compare what one had been in
those days with what one was today. She hadn't, of course,
been Kay Douglas at all then. She'd been Catherine Dun-
can, daughter of a waiter in a cheaply-flashed restaurant
off Oxford Street. Kay thought of him as a tired, hounded
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
23
creature with a little dry choky cough that never left him,
with eyes that were dull and lack-luster, with shoulders
that stooped and a chest that was queerly hollow. He used
to take her for a walk sometimes, whenever his time off
coincided with her own free hours between school and bed-
time. They'd go to Wandsworth Common and he'd tell
her of the people who came to the restaurant; of the wo-
men's pretty clothes and their jewelry, and of the men
who brought them. In tails and white ties, now and then.
Real gentry.
"Di'monds all over her she had, Kitty," Jim Duncan
would say, more because the light in her eye and her eager
parted lips encouraged him in the manufacture of Romance
rather than because he had facts on which to build his
stories. "They was just like stars. An' the way she dances.
I tell you it's a fair treat to watch her dance and no
mistake."
"I'll dance when I'm grown up," Kitty would say. "An'
I'll wear di'monds too, Pop. Real di'monds and pearls an'
all sorts of pretty stones. An' I'll wear lovely frocks with
trains, an' hats with feathers. I'm goin' to be a real lady
when I'm grown up, Pop. I'm goin' to be a nactress too,
and have my picture in the papers. What d'you have to do
to be a nactress, Pop?"
Jim Duncan didn't know, but his ignorance never wor-
ried her. She'd go on chattering, sketching wild fantastic
unbelievable plans and he let her talk. She made up, he
told himself-trudging beside her and never quite able to
forget while he walked the pain that jabbed at his chest-
for a lot. For work he hated; for a wife who drank; for
the dingy unhealthy house in the Balham side street; for
all the ideals on which he'd lived for a short time so
gloriously and to which years and years ago he'd had to
say good-by once and for all. Kitty, somehow, made up
24
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
for them a bit. Not altogether, but making life tolerable
and bearable. She was so pretty, for one thing. Pretty as
a picture with her great gray eyes and her hair that looked
just as though it had been made of spun gold. He liked to
hear her talk too and ideals didn't hurt. They didn't get
you anywhere, not in the long-run. But they didn't hurt,
and if she liked to see herself as a great actress it wasn't
for him to put a spoke in her wheel. So he let her talk,
and one year when he made a bit of money on a tip a
customer gave him for the Cambridgeshire he paid for her
to go to a dancing class. It was a secret between them be-
cause Janet Duncan wouldn't have stood for money being
spent on things of that sort. Money that was over and
above bare necessities went in gin and beer at the 'Four
Horsemen' at the corner of the street, or she knew the
reason why, so that the dancing classes had been a deadly
and rather treasured secret between them and Kitty had
done well. Her teacher had spoken quite highly of her.
"It's a pity she can't go on, Mr. Duncan. She's really
got quite a gift for dancing and she carries herself beauti-
fully. I'll be really sorry to lose her next week."
But the money only ran to one term's lessons although
when she was thirteen she did manage, following an
almost superhuman struggle, to win a scholarship that
took her to the secondary school in George Street. Her
mother hadn't wanted her to go there either. She'd said,
banging the kitchen table, interpolating her demands with
unrepeatable hideous words:
"What good'll it do her? Lazy, play actin' little minx,
that's all she is. Let her go out to work an' earn 'er keep."
She'd flounced round on her tired, coughing husband. "If
you an' 'er thinks she's goin' ter stop 'ome, idlin' like a
lady...."
It had gone on for hours and in the end Kay's teacher
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
25
had intervened, so that she took up the scholarship at last.
And at the big secondary school, mixing with girls who
were the daughters of little tradesmen, of the lower strata
of professional men, she'd watched her step carefully,
chosen her friends with care, cultivated those who could
help her, avoided like the plague those who might, in the
future, drag her down to their own level. And at sixteen,
just when she was ready to leave, her father caught a
cold and died. The doctor talked of lung trouble vaguely
without elaborating his diagnosis, and after the funeral
the Secretary of a Savings Club brought Kay a check for
eighty-two pounds and a note which her father had left
for her. The note was short and pitiful. It said: "The
money's what I've saved all my life. It's to help you to be
a lady, Kitty, so after I'm gone you go ahead and use it.
Your mother's got the Insurance so she'll be O.K. This is
yours and if you want me to lie quietly in my coffin you'll
use it."
Janet Duncan had stormed at her, beaten her, screamed
abuse at her, but Kay stuck to her guns and to the money.
She took the eighty-two pounds to the headmistress of her
school and made her request.
"I want to be an actress, a great actress, and first I want
to learn how to speak like a lady. A real lady, Miss Merri-
man. Will you take this money and teach me?"
Miss Merriman was touched by her faith and more than
a little impressed but she didn't take on the job herself.
Instead she made inquiries and as a result Kay went for
two years to the Savoy School of Dramatic Art where,
if she didn't come into contact with those women in her
own mind she called real ladies, she met girls who at least
gave a passably good imitation of the genuine article. And
where the school failed, her own natural instinct, coupled
with a genius for mimicry, a marvelously-retentive ear and
26
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
an excellent memory supplied her with what she needed.
At eighteen she got her first job. Walking on in panto-
mime. She was only paid twenty-five shillings a week, but
it enabled her to leave the hovel that went by the name of
'home' and set up for herself. It hadn't been easy because
twenty-five shillings a week doesn't permit of anything
over and above the starkly sheer necessities of life but
she'd managed it somehow, and anything, even a diet that
was only one degree removed from actual hunger, was bet-
ter than life in the squalor and degradation of that house
in the Balham side street.
But the twenty-five shillings swelled after a bit. She was
conscientious and hard working and terribly, fanatically
ambitious, and by the time she was twenty-one she was
playing small parts. A line, "Dinner is served, madam,"
or "A gentleman to speak to you please, sir." Occasionally
even two lines, and finding two pounds in her pay envelope
on Friday night. And then at twenty-one she caught a
chill and developed pneumonia. She'd had to choose be-
tween getting her shoes soled and heeled or paying the first
installment on a new evening frock and the frock had won.
One had to get clothes somehow; to look decent if one
went out dancing or to supper, and if the soles of her
shoes let in the water at least the uppers looked all right
and no one saw the soles. But she'd sat in clammy wet
stockings once too often and one night she went home
blazing hot and shivering from head to foot. She was de-
lirious next day and her landlady, who didn't want a
corpse on her hands if she could help it, telephoned for an
ambulance and had her taken to hospital. It wasn't a very
serious attack on the whole, but she'd been underfed for
years and the house doctor warned her quite definitely that
she'd have to take care, and for months afterwards she'd
had a stupid little cough that had quite effectually stepped
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
27
between her and any chance of success in the profession
she'd chosen. Managers didn't cast for decent parts girls
with silly little coughs they couldn't stop, so she'd walked
warily, dosing herself desperately with cod-liver oil, giving
up cigarettes and buying, with the money she would other-
wise have spent on them, milk instead. And two years be-
fore Bryan Onslow found her, the cough went. But after
that scare she'd gone carefully for a long time keeping to
the milk and the cod-liver oil, going to bed early, getting
all the rest she could snatch out of life, and the result had
amply justified the means. She looked the picture of radi-
ant health when Bryan Onslow took her into Dickson's
office; clear-eyed, clear-skinned, and pink-cheeked, her
bones covered snugly and deliciously with flesh that was
warm and firm and splendid. Young Onslow had com-
mented on it.
"I'm fed up with walking skeletons, Dickie. What I
want's a girl who looks human and healthy. Not a bag of
bones that rattles when it's touched."
It hadn't, however, been so easy to go steady after that.
The play flung her into the limelight, gave her friends,
brought her invitations to this party, to that and the other.
She was making thirty pounds a week now; could afford
to buy pretty clothes; to make the best of her own undeni-
able beauty and life, from being a ceaseless struggle to
keep a roof over her head and to buy enough to eat, be-
came suddenly a thing of gaiety, of admiration, of men
who vied with each other for the privilege of her favors,
of parties that followed one another in reckless, bewilder-
ing profusion so that one simply hadn't time to go to bed
early. And with champagne and caviar at one's elbow so
to speak it was only reasonable that milk and eggs and
cod-liver oil should go by the board.
It wasn't until she caught a cold and the cough came
28
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
back that she realized the ditch for which she was heading.
Dorothy Rawsden had been a stiff part; it had tired her a
lot, but it hadn't really hurt her and the strain of playing
too hard coupled with work hadn't begun to tell on her.
But A Lady of Leisure was a different proposition alto-
gether. It took out of her everything she had to give,
drained her dry, flung her like a limp rag back on excite-
ment to supply the glow nature couldn't sustain, so that
parties became a sheer mental and physical necessity. Be-
sides, parties were wonderful. It was marvelous to be
sought after, flattered, pampered. One could be tired out,
half-dead with exhaustion and then somebody came and
took one out to dance, and in the lights and the music and
the gayety one forgot how tired one was and only remem-
bered, praying that it all might last, how splendid life could
really be.
The cough, however, worried her, irritated her more
than anything else, and because, even as a star, a cough
can definitely detract from one's charm, she went to the
doctor. He was a busy young general practitioner, but he
spent nearly half an hour listening to her chest and back
and then sent her to Sir Godfrey Horton. Who only saw
people by appointment and who charged her five guineas
for doing very much the same things to her as the young
practitioner had done for ten-and-sixpence four days
earlier.
At the end of the interview he asked questions. Unemo-
tionally and almost impersonally, but laying great stress on
the accuracy of her replies. She'd often had colds? Yes.
Her father had died of "lung trouble"? Yes. She'd had
pneumonia? Yes. And it had left her with a cough? Yes
again. Yes to all the questions that, simple enough in them-
selves, seemed, asked by Sir Godfrey in that quiet un-
emotional voice, to take on suddenly a peculiar, sinister
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
29
significance. And at the end of them all, when apparently
there were no more intimate details for which he could
possibly ask, he'd warned her quite seriously. Using long
sonorous words but words that, translated into simple
everyday language meant exactly what her father's doctor
and, a few years later, the hospital house-physician had
meant too. She laughed at him then because to laugh was
obviously the only thing to do. One couldn't take things
like that seriously. One couldn't, riding as one was on the
top of the wave, pay any real attention to a man who hinted
at future lung trouble and the absolute necessity of a com-
plete rest.
"By rest," Sir Godfrey Horton had said, "I mean rest,
young lady. No late nights, no hot rooms, no stuffy at-
mospheres. If you'll take my advice you'll go out of Lon-
don altogether. Live in the fresh air all day and if pos-
sible sleep in it all night. If you'll do this, and do it consci-
entiously, I've not the slightest doubt that at the end of
that time we shall have you fit and strong again."
The instructions he'd given her were right of course.
He knew exactly what he was talking about when it came
to bodies and he didn't deal in minds. He left those to
the colleague who specialized in brain and nerves and con-
tented himself with his own bit of the human machine
which was lungs. But it was one thing to give instructions;
to say "Give it all up, go away and rest," and quite an-
other to carry the instructions into practice. One had had
so little fun. So terribly little. Life had been such a strug-
gle. One had fought tooth and nail to get a grip on the
precarious ladder of success and it was all so short. In
a few years it'd all be over anyway. You could go on act-
ing. You could be even a famous actress until you were
sixty and more but you couldn't ever be young again. Not
twenty-six with the world at your feet. With men in love
30
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
with you, falling over themselves to take you out. Sending
you flowers, calling for you in luxurious cars. Whatever
else happened, however successful you might be, youth
couldn't stay. You'd simply got to make the best of it while
it lasted. Crowd into its swiftly passing years all the fun
and gayety and splendor they would hold, and get what you
could out of life before it was too late. So that you
couldn't go away, out of it all and into the country, just
because you had a cough and a nebulous disease that bore
the frightening name of consumption, and doctors said
that fresh air with no parties and no fun might cure it.
So she'd defied the instructions and pushing Sir God-
frey's warning into the back of her mind, refusing to be
afraid, had gone to all the parties she'd been asked to
and had had a wonderful time. And when the cough per-
sisted she'd flung herself into the excitement with re-
doubled enthusiasm because excitement prevented you
from thinking of what, after all, was only the aftermath
of a silly cold on a completely exhausted body.
But if excitement stimulated her mind it did exhaust
her body. She was losing weight fast, too, and people were
beginning to comment on it.
"What are you doing, Kay? Banting? Having a love
affair?" They teased her, ragged her, blissfully uncon-
scious of the sting in their idle jests, and then at last, today,
because she was so tired she could hardly drag one foot
after the other and to be gay and bright and charming was
sheer physical torture, she'd gone back to Sir Godfrey
Horton and, faced with X-ray photographs, had learned
the truth.
"I won't," Kay said. She got up off the bed and went
over to the glass and sat before it staring at herself. "I'll
die before I'll give up. I'll go on. I'll have a good time
and make the best of things. And if I die soon, I die.
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
31
At any rate I'll have lived until then and to wear out's
better than rusting out." She took a bottle of little white
tablets from the drawer of her dressing-table, tipped out
two into the palm of her hand, swallowed them. Cora
Marsland had given them to her a couple of weeks earlier.
"They're good pick-me-ups," she'd said. "No, they're
not drugs, darling. Nothing that'll hurt you, on my honor.
But if you could take a couple of those after you're dead
I'll swear you'd get up out of your coffin to see what's
going on round you. Keep them in case you want them.
I can get lots more."
Cora was right too. They were marvelous tablets. You
felt dead. Utterly dead, so that you didn't care and noth-
ing mattered. And then, taking one of those little tablets,
you felt nothing at all for a bit until, all at once, the aches
and weariness and deadly lassitude began to slide out and
you felt grand. Ready for anything. Game to go anywhere
with anybody.
Mattie, answering her ring, stared :
"Well, you certainly look as if yer rest had done yer
good," she said. "Now, listen to me, dearie. Take things
easy. Go steady. If only you'd jest go a bit quieter now an'
agen"
"Who wants to go quieter?" Kay said. She was rum-
maging in a wardrobe, flinging a frock here, a coat there.
"Who wants to go steady? You're an old hen, Mattie. Give
me my bag and then get Lord Richard Veryan on the tele-
phone. Tell him I've changed my mind and if he likes to
come back after the show I'll go to the Silver Archway
after all. I'm perfectly all right, you know, Mattie. Be
happy while you may; you'll be dead a hell of a long time.
I'm going out to dinner. If any one wants me they can get
me after the show at the theater. Honest to goodness I'm
all right, Mattie."
.
III
IN the end the question of rest and life in the open air
was taken out of her hands. London, in January, was full
of influenza. Beginning with an odd case here and there
the thing raced to epidemic heights. Whole families were
in bed at the same time with it, died of it. The papers car-
ried flaming headlines. "Influenza deaths growing."
"Shortage of doctors." Theaters and cinemas were half-
empty. Shows, for which heretofore you couldn't get a seat
unless you booked it four or five days ahead, played to
houses sprinkled here and there with people where there
had been serried rows.
London reeked of camphor and Vapex and every known
and unknown disinfectant and preventative. There were
notices in trams and buses and public buildings telling peo-
ple what to do and what not to do, and along the roads
that led to Golders Green, to Kensal Rise, to Paddington
Cemetery, you met constant processions of funerals.
Cora Marsland caught it. Compelled at last and only
then after a desperate struggle to hold her own to give
her eager little understudy the chance for which she'd
waited so longingly. And a week later, while Cora con-
valesced, the understudy died. Whole choruses mysteri-
ously changed, managers and stage managers were frantic,
and in the last week of January Kay got it.
She knew she was in for it when she went to the theater.
Her head ached intolerably, her hands were sticky and hot
32
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
33
even while she shivered and shook with cold. Mattie said:
"You're a plain fool, Miss. You can't play tonight. You
aint fit."
"I'm all right," Kay said. Her voice sounded queer and
unreal. As though it didn't, for some obscure reason, be-
long to her. "And I've got to go on. Even if I do feel rot-
ten. Miss Desborough went to hospital this morning. I'll
get through all right, Mattie."
Tony Seabright, coming round to her dressing-room
after the first act, was aghast.
"You ought to be in bed. You're simply blazing with
fever. I'm going to tell Richardson."
"I'm all right," Kay said again. She spoke doggedly,
determinedly. If you kept on repeating it perhaps it came
true in the end. There was a man, a Frenchman-what
on earth was his name? He'd a theory that if you went on
and on telling yourself that certain things would happen
they happened. He'd a silly slogan. She wished childishly
and with an absurd desperation that she could remember
his name. Its absence from her mind took on suddenly an
aspect of tremendous vital importance. "You and Mattie
ought to be twins, Tony. You both fuss so. I tell you it's
all right, darling." She added spitefully and entirely in-
voluntarily, shocked beyond measure at the sound of her
own words. "You don't have to be afraid I'll let you down
or cramp your style."
She saw Tony's eyes narrow and darken as they always
did when he was hurt, felt an instant urge to tell him
she'd never meant to say the beastly thing, that nothing
had been farther from her mind, opened her mouth to
apologize and suddenly decided it was too much trouble.
She didn't want to apologize or to talk. To Tony or to any
one. She wished he'd go away. Stay away. Do anything.
Only leave her alone. If he didn't she'd scream in a minute.
34
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
Shriek in helpless hysteria, beat at the air with impotent,
frantic hands. She felt awful. Every bone in her body was
a separate and individual torture, yanking at her. Jabbing
knives into her. And her head was like a cauldron filled
with boiling lead.
A call boy tapped at her door. The sound went through
her like the reverberation of heavy artillery.
"Five minutes, Miss Douglas."
She said, because to say something seemed imperative:
"I wish you'd clear out and let me have five minutes'
peace, Tony. I've got a blinding headache, that's all. I'm
sorry if I'm a beast, but if I could just have five min-
utes"
The second act seemed interminable. Once, from her
place by the stage fireplace, she caught a glimpse of the
S.M. in the wings. She thought he looked worried and
then forgot him in the agony of trying to make convinc-
ing a part that seemed, all at once, to be composed of noth-
ing but a series of aimless lines punctuated by the torture
of jabbing knives.
At the end of the act the S.M. spoke to her. His voice
was short, not from want of sympathy but because he was
worried to death. This damned 'flu and Kay looked
ghastly. If she went down, with the Desborough girl al-
ready in hospital Who the hell would be an S.M. any-
way? All the worry and bother and none of the kudos. If
things went wrong, who got the blame? Who stood the
racket? The people who deserved it? Not by a long shot.
That lay on the shoulders of the S.M. and now if Kay
went down? He touched her shoulder as she passed
him, his voice edgy with the irritation he couldn't hide.
"You all right, Miss Douglas?"
"Yes," Kay said, and then, with an irritation that
matched his own, turned and spat at him. "What's the
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
35
matter with you all anyway? Am I putting up a rotten
show? First Tony and now you. Fussing. Asking idiotic
questions. Unless I'm rotten-what's the matter with you?
Can't I have a headache without the whole town fussing
round like a lot of worried hens?"
Richardson said pacifically:
"That's O.K., Miss Douglas. Sorry. It's only well,
this 'flu's getting past a joke. Glad you're all right any-
way."
The third act was a nightmare. It went on and on and
on. Endlessly. And out front people were apathetic. With
thoughts that persisted in wandering. One couldn't, even
watching a first-rate show, forget altogether that all round
one people were dying. By tens and dozens and twenties,
and that at any moment almost one might oneself be play-
ing the star part in a silent movie of one's own. One came
to the theater because some one said some fool thing
about "mind over health" and "depression being the sure
way to foster germs" but, while one had to admire a clever
play and really brilliant acting, one couldn't be as enthusi-
astic over them both as one would have been under other
less trying conditions. To Kay, fighting with her back to
the wall to put over an emotion that had quite suddenly
lost all its importance and become as nothing before the
battery of slashing knives and the bubbling of the boiling
lead in her head, it seemed as though the act would never
end. Then with a sort of gasp of surprise and relief she
heard Tony's closing lines, saw the curtains swing to-
gether, part, swing again, part for her curtain with Tony,
took it somehow, took another by herself, and with the
first bars of the National Anthem drumming into her ears
turned to go to her dressing-room, saw the stage begin to
swing with a steady rhythmic movement round and round
her head, saw everything going black in front of her blaz-
36
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
ing eyes, and heard, out of the mists as she crashed for-
ward, Tony's voice:
"Look out. Get out of the way. Don't you see? Rich-
ardson-Maybury get a doctor for God's sake. And get
him quick."
IV
THEY kept her in the nursing home for nearly a month.
The doctor summoned by Richardson had been shocked
out of even his own professional calm when he sounded
her chest and lungs and when, contrary to all the laws of
nature, she turned the corner and began laboriously to
climb the upward slope of convalescence he talked to her
with desperate urgency and without taking the trouble to
disguise, by long words, the facts he meant to ram home.
He was a nice young fellow, earnest, terribly in love with
his profession, desperately eager to heal, whenever healing
was humanly possible, the battered wrecks that came
under his care. He said:
"I'm not going to beat about the bush with you, Miss
Douglas. Your chest"
"I know," Kay said. She was a thin washed-out rail
and her eyes were like blue saucers set in a bed of thickly
applied soot but she looked oddly rested. She'd been lying
in bed three weeks now. With nothing to do but to sleep,
to drink the things they gave her to drink, to eat, apatheti-
cally, the food they brought her to eat, and queerly enough
she was quite content to be there. The theater and all that
the theater meant seemed like something that belonged to
another world. You thought of it, of course, now that you
had nothing to do but to lie in bed and think, just as you
thought of the parties and the wonderful times you'd had,
but without the slightest desire to renew acquaintance with
37
38
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
any of them. All you wanted in these days apparently was
to lie still and do nothing. To be washed and looked after
just as though you were a baby, to be waited on hand and
foot, to go to sleep when you wanted to sleep, to be thor
oughly lazy and idle and slack. Of course you wouldn't al-
ways be like that. At least you supposed you wouldn't.
There'd come a time when you'd be well again and want
things just as you'd wanted them before. Success and ad-
miration and parties. Dancing and night clubs. All the
gaiety and fun that went to the making up of life, but
just now It was lovely to lie in bed and know you
could go on lying there. In peace and rest and utter, mirac-
ulous comfort. It was even nice to listen to this eager boy
who made of your miserable body a thing of such tre-
mendous personal importance. Propped up with pillows,
surrounded by the flowers people had sent her, with, on the
table beside her, a little bowl piled high with cards of in-
quiry, she smiled at him with lips that were beginning to
smile again without apparently any effort. "You don't
have to break things to me, Doctor. Sir Godfrey Horton
told me all about it months ago."
"Then you ought to be ashamed of yourself," David
Armstrong said shortly. "If you knew, there's no excuse
for you at all. What d'you suppose you were given life
for? And a beautiful body?" Listening to him, Kay knew
instinctively that as a woman her body didn't interest him.
It was beautiful and he knew it but purely from the view-
point of his profession. She thought idly: "He's the only
impersonal person I've ever met. It's my body as a part
of his job he admires because he sees in it something to
heal and make perfect." She heard his angry impatient
voice breaking across her lazy mind, upbraiding her. "You
women make me sick. If you were given a valuable jewel
you'd take care of it. You'd lock it up and treasure it and
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
39
guard it although if it's lost or damaged there's always
another to take its place. It may cost money and it mayn't
be easy to replace but somewhere there is one to replace it.
And yet you treat life which is the greatest treasure that's
ever existed, that's something given you by God to take
care of for Him and which can't ever be replaced no mat-
ter where you try to find it, as though it were a toy bought
at Woolworth's. For threepence. To be played with and
tossed about and ignored and ill-treated. My God, you
make me sick."
"You talk," Kay said, stung for a moment into argu-
ment and resentment, "as though our lives weren't our
own."
"They're not," David Armstrong said. He got up,
shoved his stethoscope into his bag, shrugged his coat
more closely about his thick, square shoulders, turned at
the door to deliver a parting shot. "Ever heard of the
claims God has on your life, Miss Douglas?"
Kay lay still for a long time after he'd gone. In her
world, of course, he'd have been ridiculed, laughed to
scorn, regarded as a fanatic. And avoided, because one
didn't like people who took life seriously and talked of
God as a personal, tangible Being. That somewhere there
was a mysterious, inexplicable Something to which one
referred lightly as God one admitted of course. One had
been brought up as a child to believe in that sort of God
but not in a personal Deity who held one responsible for
such a thing as one's own life. She thought, resentment still
lingering: "I wish he'd leave me alone. After all, if I
wanted to live my own way"
He came again next day, nagging at her with dogged
persistence.
"If you follow the plan I'll lay down for you the chances
are you'll be cured. I don't say definitely you will be cured,
40
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
because no doctor if he's honest can say that, but there's
a chance. It'll be dull, deadly dull, but it won't be as
ghastly as the end of your life'll be if you go on as you're
going. I went to see Horton last night. No business of
mine, of course, except that as a patient I'm terribly inter-
ested in you. It's no compliment to you personally, because
I'd be equally interested in a mangy dog if it happened
to be brought to me for attention. I'm a doctor not because
it's one way of earning a living but because I can't think
of a grander job than curing the bodies men and women
have wrecked through, in nine cases out of ten, their own
consummate folly. Anyway, to make a long story short,
Horton told me what I wanted to know and I shan't rest
till I've made you see reason. If you take care now”
"Yes," Kay said. "Go to a hospital. I know. Sir God-
frey told me all that. I don't want a hospital. If I'm going
to die I'll die and I hope I'll die without whining or griz-
zling. But while I'm alive I'll live. And make the best of
things."
"Unless you've a lot of money or you're coward enough
to commit suicide when the fun's over you'll find it won't
be so easy to make the best of things," David Armstrong
said grimly. "Ever been to a T.B. Sanatorium? Where
people who haven't got money come to die? Who, because
they aren't cowards, have to drag on year after year, know-
ing exactly what the end's going to be? Have you?"
"You're trying to frighten me," Kay said. She twisted
restlessly under the neat white bed-linen. "It isn't fair."
"It's perfectly fair and I am trying to frighten you. If
I can frighten you enough to make you realize the crazy
folly of what you are doing I'll have done something worth
doing. You're young. You've got a chance. Give yourself
two years, three years, and there's every hope you'll be able
to come back to all the things you think mean life. Not,
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
41
certainly, to live as you've been living but at least able to
work. Able to do your job decently and effectually and
that's something. As things are now, how long d'you sup-
pose you'll be able to carry on at the rate you've been going
lately?"
Kay's mind ran back swiftly, with terrible clarity, to the
cupboard in her dressing-room. It had been full of little
bottles and boxes and tubes. You couldn't chance cough-
ing on the stage. You had to take care about that so that
every day, because the cough was growing daily more tire-
some and irritating, you tried something new to stop it.
And while new things at first were good their ability to
effect any real cure always ended sooner or later. Listening
to this angry impatient young man you couldn't help re-
membering these rows of bottles and the question "How
long?" that had always been cropping up in your mind,
however desperately you tried to crush it out of existence.
"You don't know me," she said. "How can you talk
about the way I've been living? You don't know anything
about it. Or about me. You see me here"
"Don't be a fool," David Armstrong said crossly.
"You're a public character, my good girl. I may be an
overworked G.P. but occasionally I find time to amuse my-
self and everybody knows Kay Douglas. You're a mag-
nificent actress apart from being a lovely woman. At least,"
he added with his passion for honesty, "you would be a
lovely woman if you gave yourself half a chance and got
some flesh on your bones. And it's because you're what you
are, young, brilliant, beautiful, that I can't stand by and
see you making such a damned fool of yourself. If you go
on as you're doing now you'll last perhaps a year. That's
brutal but it's true, and at the end of that time you'll be a
pitiful wreck, the more pitiful because you'll have brought
the end on yourself. If you do as I tell you there's every
42
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
hope in the world for you to come back to it. To your job
at all events, and unless you're a fool you'll see the force
of what I say. I can send you to a sanatorium you can

When you've finished with that you can go into the coun-

"Yes," Kay said bitterly. "And who'll want me? Who'll

Don't be silly, my dear girl."
She went to the Sanatorium of course. There was noth-
ing else to do really and, feeling as one did, like a limp
exhausted rag, it was easier to take the line of least re-

kind, because through their kindness it was impossible not
to recognize pity. You knew they were saying: "Poor girl,
it's hard luck, but of course she's finished. Completely
done." They all talked with unfailing cheeriness saying
very much the same thing:
"We'll miss you terribly, darling, but you'll soon be
back. And we'll run down as often as we possibly can to
see you. Do take care of yourself, Kay."
They brought her flowers and chocolates, books and
little flimsy jackets to wear in bed, but only a few of them
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
43
kissed her when they left and those who did pecked at her
gingerly. She thought: "They're afraid. I'm infectious and
they're afraid. It's just as well I'm going. Out of it all.
Once they'd learned the truth they'd all have cried off.
They don't want illness about. They've no use for it, and
you can't blame them. It's just as well I'm going because
away from it, I shan't at least have to watch them trying
to avoid me."
Tony came the day before she left the nursing home.
Tomorrow she would be going to the Sanatorium. David
Armstrong was to take her himself. To see her installed
there. He was terribly interested in her, far keener on her
recovery than she was herself, and he was going to drive

no time there, my dear girl." And now, on the last day,

"They take your mind off things," he explained. "Cross-

minimize or make light of what he honestly thought was
a colossal, overwhelming tragedy. "When I get fed up
with things and sorry for myself"
Kay said, interrupting him:
"Are you ever sorry for yourself, Tony?"
"Yes," Tony said. He got up from his chair by the fire
in her tidy little bedroom, went across to the window and
stood staring down into the roadway below. "I'm alınost
44
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
sorrier for myself at this moment than I am for you and

"I can't," Kay said again. She took her hand from his

SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
45
top one was little and very soft. The sort of pillow seen

SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
"I shan't," Kay said. She clung to him, crying a little.
He was the last link with everything that made life worth

V
SHE stayed in the Sanatorium for just over a year, dur-
ing which time she lived, ate and slept, winter and summer,
rain, hail, snow or shine, in a hut in the garden; ate three
square meals and drank three pints of milk a day; went to
bed at eight-thirty and got up at ten; walked specified dis-
tances, read for specific periods; did, grumbling and re-
sentful at first, later because to obey orders became more
or less second nature, the things she was told to do and
left undone the things she was forbidden to do. And while,
for the first few weeks the system irked her unbearably,
bored her to extinction so that again and again she wished
herself dead and out of it once and for all, there came a
time when, feeling sounder and healthier in mind as well
as in body, she knew she could afford to stick it out and
give herself the chance David Armstrong had wanted her
to have. It was, of course, unutterably dull. Deadly mo-
notonous because day after day, week in week out, the
routine never varied. You were overhauled, weighed,
tested, asked questions, given instructions. You were made
to rest so many hours, to eat so much, to drink so much,
to walk so far. Your weight was written down on a chart
together with your temperature, your pulse, your respira-
tion. The staff of nurses, brisk, capable, efficient, left
nothing to chance or to memory. Every hour of your day
was tabulated, accounted for, every function of your body
commented on, written down on the chart that, like a
47
48
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
familiar spirit, followed you wherever you went. At first
you grumbled, stormed, cursed, but it wasn't any use. No
one paid the slightest attention to the storming. You were
regarded by the staff very much as though you were a
peevish, fractious child but you got out of nothing by
making a fuss and a disturbance and in the end you gave
it up. Powerless, despite sulks, tantrums, grousings, to
make the slightest impression on the calm unruffled sur-
face of the staff's unfailing and unshakable patience and
tolerance.
And as time went by you certainly felt stronger. You
might grudge making the admission while you felt savage
and sore and resentful, but you had to admit the truth
sooner or later. It was rather wonderful really to wake up
in the morning and find yourself taking an active interest
in breakfast; to discover that milk wasn't as repulsive as
you had previously thought it; to realize that with rest of
body came also an odd rest of mind. You felt sore, of
course. That was inevitable. Sore and bitterly, hideously
resentful. God was so cruel. He flung you in the world
with nothing, and then stood by watching. He let you
fight and struggle, bite and scratch, and kick your way to
the top of the ladder, allowed you to sit there dangling
your legs in the glorious golden sunshine of success and
then, without a word of explanation, for no fault of your
own, He picked you up and dropped you back into the
bottomless pit of despair and utter, hopeless misery.
It was all very fine for David Armstrong to talk, to
chortle over the way you were putting on weight, to gloat
with devilish satisfaction, "There, what did I say. Didn't
I tell you so?" David Armstrong was only interested in
your body. In the ounces and pounds and stones chalked
up on the damnable chart that followed you round the
little circumscribed world into which you had been flung.
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
49
Weight, and what he called "general reaction," were all
he cared to talk about. It didn't matter to him that you
had lost your place in the sun. That, to acquire the ounces
and pounds and stones by which he and the resident doc-
tor and apparently the entire nursing staff set such store,
you had had to give up everything you treasured and
valued and held dear in the world. It was all very fine, too,
to say that people wouldn't forget you. In theory that
sounded pretty and very nice, but it wasn't true. It had
been true for a time, for a month. Even, in some cases,
for two months. Richard Veryan had motored twice to
Amberley in those first few weeks of her incarceration.
He arrived laden with flowers, with fruit, with chocolates
in marvelous boxes; with, the second time, a ridiculous
toy dog that barked with tremendous realism every time
you squeezed its tail. He'd said: "Come back to us soon,"
with a note of real tenderness in his voice, but, though
he'd written three or four times after, charming letters
that she'd read again and again and again, he'd only been
to see her just twice. And lately he hadn't even written.
He was rumored to be terribly in love with Rachel For-
dray; to be always about with her; to be even engaged to
her. In any case he'd forgotten Kay, as for that matter,
most of the crowd had forgotten her after a bit. Cora
Marsland had been to Amberley once. She'd arrived with
a rather noisy crew who'd talked and laughed too fast and
too loudly, and Matron had found it necessary to ask them
to be a little quieter, reminding them that while Kay was
more or less convalescent there were lots of other occu-
pants of other little huts who weren't, and who couldn't
stand noise. So they hadn't come any more. It was the
same with all of the old crowd with the exception, of
course, of Tony. They'd been fond of her after their fash-
ion. Really fond of her, but in their affection they'd no
50
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
room for illness or trouble or death. They wanted gaiety
and life and parties and where none of them were to be
found, they just didn't go. It wasn't callousness or indif-
ference, because they didn't give themselves the chance
to be either. They just put out of their minds the things
they didn't want to remember. When Kay couldn't take
part in the things that interested them, when she lay in
bed surrounded by terrible, ghostly wrecks of what had
been human beings, they put her out too. One didn't blame
them. It was, after all, only what one had done oneself,
and Death wasn't pretty. There were times, of course,
when one had to face him, to listen to what he had to say,
but one didn't, if one had any sense at all, run after him
trying to make friends with him. So one by one they for-
got to write, forgot to send the gifts with which they'd
inundated her in the early days of her illness, pushed her
back to where, in their flimsy little minds, she belonged.
Amongst the dead and rusty ashes of things they didn't
want, and in time did as she'd known they would do and
forgot her.
All, of course, except Tony. Tony, bless him, never
forgot. Never let one slip for a moment, apparently, out
of his mind. He came down practically every Sunday,
tearing along the Great West Road in his smart little
sports car, arriving in time for lunch with all sorts of
marvelous things from Fortnum & Mason's that they ate
together in the little garden hut. Matron and the staff
adored Tony. He made shameless love to all of them and
they did things for him they didn't do for any one else.
Let him stay and have meals when one of the rules was
that visitors mustn't have meals with patients; brought
him out special sterilized china and spoons and forks;
made excuses to hang around while he told Kay ridiculous
impossible stories of ridiculous impossible happenings.

51
And he was always the same. Like a devoted, faithful dog.
Kay said once :
"It's so unfair of me to let you do this, Tony. I ought

"Thanks," Tony said comfortably. It was mid-summer

He told her, too, the things she longed to know. Bits
of the back stage gossip she loved.
"Richardson and Babs Desborough had the hell of a
row last Tuesday, Kay. She kept the stage, and he told

That's what he told her amongst other things and it didn't
please her either. She's not bad for an afterthought, but
she can't carry success you know. It goes to her head."
Or:
"Muriel Vinton's chucked Bill Dawsbury. He's chasing

Or again:

that. Says he'll have a corking part for you soon as you're
ready to play it."
They could talk for hours. He never attempted to go
back until after lunch on Monday unless he'd a call for
52
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
rehearsal on Monday morning, and then, after lunch, he'd

But he was the only real link with the glories of the
past. After the first two or three months no one but Tony
bothered her at all. They didn't mean to be cruel, but they
simply hadn't time to remember, that was all, and in the
end Kay made up her mind to the inevitable. All the glory
and wonder of the past was over. Done with. Finished.
She was one of the Also Rans now and she'd have to make
the best of it. To take a back seat in the world. To accept,
gratefully, the crumbs that came her way. Later, when she
was fit again, there'd be small parts. Enough to keep her
at all events, to stand between her and actual want. Ben-
stein and Peterson and Jeffreys would have filled her place
but they'd give her something and with that, in future,
she'd have to make do. In the meantime she'd got enough
to live on quietly. She was glad now she hadn't listened
when Cora Marsland wanted her to launch out, to take a
bigger flat, to employ a smarter maid than old Mattie, to
buy a car of her own instead of hiring taxis or traveling
in buses. What she had wasn't much certainty but it was
better than nothing. By going very steadily and slowly,
by watching every step and every penny, she could make
it last out somehow. There'd be no luxuries, of course.
Nothing that even resembled luxury in any shape or form,
but with great care it could at least be made to stretch out
and to stand between herself and actual want until the
time came when she could start work again. And in the
meantime it was no use fretting. No use crying any longer
over spilt milk. One had simply to carry on somehow, any-
how. To realize that the past was over and done with, to
make the best of what was left with as good a grace as
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
53
one could muster. To give up one's mind, for the time

and did as she was told, getting stronger with the passing

"We've done our work, Miss Douglas. And, if you'll let

when you've ceased to need it" He shrugged thin

know where you're going I'll write to the local G.P. and
tell him to keep a watchful eye on you, though I honestly
don't think that, with ordinary care, you'll need him.
You've been an excellent patient. You've helped yourself

as much as we've been able to help you, and I don't have

VI
IT was Tony Seabright, coming down on the Sunday fol-
lowing the announcement of her release, who told her
about the cottage in Cornwall and about Mrs. Poldreath.
He'd been down there for the first time ages ago and had
gone back since, again and again. He said:
"It's a heavenly place to be quiet in, Kay. With nothing
but the sea and the rocks and the gulls and a fisherman
or two. You can sort of catch up with yourself in a place
like that. Get even with things if you know what I mean.
And Mrs. Poldreath's fine. She feeds you like a fighting
cock. Gives you great plates of hot splits and jam and
honest-to-God Cornish cream. Bowls of cream. It's the
very place for you to go to, Kay.
"I feel so lost," Kay said. "Like a homeless dog, with
no place anywhere that really belongs to me. It's horrid,
Tony. When everything's so impermanent. When there's
nothing fixed or settled. Yesterday I half made up my
mind to come back to work. After all, what does it matter?
A year more or less. I wish sometimes I'd never let Dr.
Armstrong talk me into coming here. If I'd gone on I'd
have been quietly dead by now probably. With all the
worry over. There's nothing you see, Tony. Nothing to
look forward to. Ever. And I was so keen. So ambitious.
All my life I've had one goal ahead of me. I've fought-
you don't know how I've fought to make good, and now
it's all over and there's nothing left. And the past's been
55
56
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
so short. If I'd had years behind me to remember-happy
years-I could bear it. But there's so little. Three fat
years out of all my life and I'm twenty-seven."
"You'll come back," Tony Seabright said. "What's the
good of letting things get you like this, Kay? In another
year, two years, you'll be fit as a fiddle. What's to stop
you then coming back?"
"Everything," Kay said flatly. "You know it too, Tony.
Girls like me, who've been just a flash in the pan, don't
make a successful come-back. If I'd had twenty years be-
hind me there'd have been a chance for me. People would
have remembered. But three years it's the same with
everything. We can all remember a long hard winter but
we forget the two marvelous spring days that broke it up
in the middle. There's no come-back, not as you and I
mean it, for girls like me, Tony."
Tony knew, even while he ridiculed the things she said,
that they were true. People forget so soon the little
meteors that flashed across the theatrical sky. They could
remember well enough the old fixed stars. Their places
were kept bright and aired and ready for them. Take
Hugh Annesley. He'd been off the stage seven years.
Dying, rumor said, of some terrible disease, shut away
from everything, and look what had happened only two
years ago. Quite suddenly a new doctor had hit on a
marvelous cure. Hugh Annesley had gone to Germany for
treatment, a dying man to all intents and purposes, and
he'd come back cured, ready to take up his job, to go back
to the niche he'd held in the hearts of an admiring public.
And what a come-back he'd had. People had risen in the
stalls shouting their welcome, roaring it. At the end of
the show he'd had to make a speech, and the whole theater
had sung "For he's a jolly good fellow" with a spon-
taneous emotion that left no doubt anywhere as to their
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
57
real feelings. He was a familiar figure to them all, one
of the stars by which they'd guided their paths, who'd set
for a time but now had flashed back, brilliantly, before
their eyes. But he'd had twenty years of theater life be-
hind him. His name had been placarded all over London
and the Provinces almost as long as the present generation
could remember, so that for him a come-back meant quite
a different thing than it would mean for Kay. It was all
very fine to talk, to be optimistic and hopeful and cheer-
ful, but at the bottom one knew quite definitely that a
come-back for Hugh Annesley, beloved and admired for
twenty years, and a come-back for Kay, a brilliant in-
finitesimal flash in a pan where there are almost ceaseless
brilliant flashes, wouldn't have anything in common.
Although, of course, one couldn't say so. One couldn't,
loving her as one did, agree with her, admit she was speak-
ing the truth if by a lie here and there one could help her
to fight a little harder against the almost insuperable odds
that frightened her so terribly.
So he ridiculed her fears, laughing at them, peering at
them. Inventing things that weren't true, elaborating those
that had a substratum of truth on which to build them.
"Collingwood's still asking for you, Kay. He says he's
never seen a girl yet who could have played Brenda as
you played it."
Or:
"Jimmy Overton stopped me last week. He wanted to
know if you'd read a play and see what you thought about
the lead in it. He'd heard you were well enough to come
back, you see. I told him you couldn't do it yet and he
said he'd let the play slide for a bit because he didn't know
any one else who could really make a success of it as you
could."
Jimmy Overton hadn't, of course, said quite that. What
58
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
he had said was: "I wish to God Kay Douglas was back.
The play's got holes in it and she could give it what it
needs to cover 'em up. I guess I'll give it the go-by. It's not
so good but that I can't find something better anyway."
And Collingwood had said: "It's damned hard luck on
that Douglas girl you know, Tony. She's clever but of
course she's over. By the time she comes back somebody'll
have found her shoes and made 'em fit. Life's like that,
my lad."
But one couldn't tell the truth to Kay. Not the bare
unvarnished truth like that, so one took a basis of fact
and on it built up any sort of story that would help to
make life a little more bearable for the present, a little
less terrifying for the future. And seeing Kay's eyes when
one told the story made it much more than just worth
while. It was hell, loving her as one did, plain hell to have
to sit by and watch her in torture knowing oneself im-
potent, incapable of doing a single solitary thing to make
life less ghastly for her. To make it a little happier and
a little more hopeful and encouraging.
She went back to London for two days prior to going
down to Cornwall. There were things she needed, things
to do, and back there again one could see for oneself a
little, sense things more or less, and see how one really
stood in one's own world, or rather in the world that had
once been one's own and of which one was becoming so
rapidly just a hanger-on. She wrote Mattie, who was liv-
ing with a married sister in Clapham since the flat had
been closed, to bring her up some of the clothes she was
taking care of for her. Heavy country clothes, her sun-
bathing suit, the things she'd worn on the long ago days
she'd spent with Richard Veryan when she'd gone with
him for week-ends at his home in Leicestershire. They
would be out of date of course, but it wouldn't matter. Not
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
59
down there in the wilds of Cornwall. Nothing mattered
very much really when one came to think of it. One just
went on living because one had a natural prejudice against
dying before one's time; dying that is, by one's own
hand, but nothing really mattered very much. Certainly
not that one's clothes were a year old and weren't the very
last and latest word in up-to-date smartness and chic.
Mattie, of course, was delighted to see her and amazed

"Glory to goodness, Miss Kay, I'd never 'ave believed

no mistake. Now you mind an' keep it up, dearie. Don't
you go an' let things get 'old of you the way they did be-
fore. Now you're well you keep well, Miss Kay."

Madam. What name, please? Miss Duncan. I beg pardon,
Madam-Miss Douglas. Yes, Madam, thank you. Good
afternoon, Madam." The cold impersonal voice hit Kay

SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
61

So she bought a frock from Simonie in Bond Street,

It wasn't, however, as easy as she thought it would be

mustn't be this or that. It became suddenly a thing of tre-

which would swing all the future events of her life. And,
being so, one could leave nothing to chance. Run no risks
of mistakes.
Later, dressed and waiting for Tony to call for her, she
