SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS 71
the crowd, had been wonderful once. Marvelous. The
things one lived for, crowding sense and sensibility into
the background, but now all at once everything had
changed. One was out of it. Sitting on the edge of a circus
ring watching people do their bit. With no part whatever
in the bit oneself any more. It wouldn't last, of course.
One couldn't go on sitting on the outside looking on and
not minding being left out. There'd come a time when one
would hate it again just as violently as one had hated it a
year ago. Resenting one's forced inactivity, loathing the
peace and quietness to which ill-health had condemned one.
Clutching, with frantic desperate hands, at any straw that
would bring one back to it. But now-one was tired prob-
ably. And, because one was tired and flat, peace and quiet
and absence of racket seemed terribly desirable. It was
funny to realize how eagerly one was looking forward to
those weeks in the country. How glad one would be to get
on the train tomorrow morning and to get away again.
Funny because, until a year ago, quiet had been the last
thing one wanted. One had hated even going to bed,
grudging terribly the hours one spent in sleep because
youth and time went so fast. And then, all at once, one
didn't grudge them any more but looked forward to them
instead, welcoming the peace and quietness that made rest
and sleep possible.

understand in the first place. Illness and death didn't

One wanted to live. To have a wild, gay time. To forget

SHE went alone to the station next morning. Tony had
a rehearsal call for ten o'clock, but leaving her the night
before he'd slipped an envelope into her hand.
"Don't mind it, will you, Kay? There's so little you'll
let me do and I'd like to do so much. You'll make me ter-
ribly happy if you'll let me give you this."
The envelope held a first-class ticket to Penzance and
seeing it Kay wanted to cry. Tony was so thoughtful,
asked so little, took what came his way so thankfully. And
she'd accepted the ticket with real gratitude.
"You're so good, Tony," she told him, and suddenly
pulled his face down to hers and kissed him. "If only
things were different. Life's the devil of a tangle, my
dear."
Paddington next morning had the unnatural look com-
mon to all big stations on Sundays. Here and there on the
long, deserted platform odd isolated figures of men and
women waited for trains. Most of the men carried golf
clubs and wore plus-fours, the women their best clothes.
Looking, one couldn't help thinking, a little self-conscious
and conspicuous in the vast emptiness of the deserted sta-
tion. Only one platform was busy. People who went to
Cornwall around Easter-time, the porter volunteered, went
early. It wasn't worth their while going all that way for
less than a week or ten days, and the train was all but full.
He found a corner seat in a first-class carriage, slid her

SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
suit-case on the rack above her head, touched his cap in

If one didn't forget one would go mad. Even before one
died. At twenty-seven to be faced with Death was so un-
fair. Unfair even to those who'd had twenty-seven glori-
ously happy years. Doubly, hideously unfair when out of
the twenty-seven, only three had been even remotely worth
the trouble of living at all.
The elder of the two women fussed continuously. Safe-
guarding herself against draughts, against smuts, against
cinders that might find her eyes. She was, Kay decided,
the girl's mother. They had the same clean-cut line of jaw,
the same eyes, blue, and terribly cold, reminding one of
the blue of icebergs under sunny water. The man was the
girl's husband obviously. He tried his best to be polite to
both women, to stifle, behind courtesy, the boredom that
was written all over him, to hide his impatient dislike of
the fuss they made over trifles. Once, catching Kay's look,
reading in it sympathy and understanding, he flushed
dully, uncomfortably, but his eyes were wretched.
Kay thought:
"Life's funny. He's got all the things I haven't got.
Money, birth, social position, health-he's the healthiest
looking creature I've seen for weeks all the things that
ought to make for happiness. And yet he isn't happy.
None of them are any happier than I am. They've got a
different cause, but they're all wretched at the bottom.
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
75
Life's funny if you stop to think about it. I suppose if
we only knew the truth there's always a fly in the oint-
ment somewhere."
There didn't, however, appear to be any fly in the oint-
ment Mrs. Poldreath provided. A boy met the train at
Penzance. He had eyes the color of brown chrysan-
themums, a shock of dark, curling hair, skin tanned
leather-brown by sun and wind and salt sea air, and a
wide shy smile that was tremendously attractive. He waited
till the crowd cleared, till Kay standing alone beside her
suit-case felt lonely and lost, and then came up and spoke
to her.
"It'll be you that's for Mrs. Poldreath's I reckon. I
brought the car in for you, Miss, if you'll follow me."
He swung ahead carrying the heavy suit-case as though
it had been a pound of tea, held open the door of an open
touring car, put the suit-case beside her on the back seat
and climbed, himself, into the driving seat. They swung
out of the station up a deserted steeply-sloping main street,
through a bottle-neck deathtrap into the open country.
The road was narrow, twisting and terrifying, but the
hedges were a marvel. Kay, who was a Cockney and had
never known that any countryside could resemble, even
faintly, the breath-taking loveliness of a Cornish lane in
spring, called to him to stop.
"Wait, it's too lovely. I've got to stop and look. D'you
mean," she caught the expression in the boy's eyes, reading
it aright. "D'you mean all these, all this, is just wild?
Just growing like this, always?"
The boy nodded, slipping in the clutch.
"We don't give much thought to it, Miss," he said.
"'Tis just what do come year by year. Nuthin' unusual
like to such as we."
The bluebells stretched as far as one could see. They
76
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
grew on the slopes of the hedges and, like stately little
sentinels clad in sky-blue helmets, in serried rows along
the tops of grass-faced walls, swayed now and then by
the ghost of a wind from the south. And with the blue-
bells were primroses, not in isolated clumps but growing
like a pale golden carpet, broken here and there by masses
of rose-red campion. Kay said in whispered ecstasy:
"It's unreal. It's like something one reads about. An
enchanted land. I can't believe it's true. Things like this."
The boy smiled at her, delightfully, showing two rows
of white, even teeth. It was clear he had heard it all be-
fore, was accustomed to the rhapsodies of "foreigners"
who came from "England” to rave over the beauties and
mysticisms and cruelties of his native country.
They passed farms and cottages, granite-built, bleak,
cold, austere, and a little forbidding. One must live in
Cornwall, belong to her by right of birth before one can
really see friendliness in the barren gray walls of her
houses, the cheerlessness of their building contrasting
oddly with the blaze of color in their gardens. Kay,
drenched in their beauty, thought:
"All the flowers in the world are here. Every color that
has ever been. There is no place, no place as beautiful as
this."
They swung left, on a terrifying hairpin bend at the top
of a hill and at the wheel the boy jerked his head forward
in the direction of a shimmering blue sea. He said, with
the true Cornishman's paucity of words:
"'Tis Polpendor here now, Miss," and steadying the
car down the narrow tortuous lane, crossed a stream
spanned by an infinitesimal bridge, brought her to a
creaking standstill outside a granite cottage and whistled.
"If you'll go indoors I'll bring your bag in."
Tea was waiting for her when she came downstairs
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
77
after washing her hands and straightening her hair. Laid
in a tiny room on a round table before a fire. Tea in a
brown teapot covered by a patchwork cosy. Home-made
splits, home-made jam, a great slab of golden-yellow
farm butter, home-made cakes and bread. Kay had never
seen such a tea. She said:
"It looks delicious, but I'm afraid you see, I don't eat
much at tea-time. I'm afraid you've been to a lot of
trouble for nothing."
Mrs. Poldreath had heard all that before. Ladies from
London all said the same thing. They didn't eat this and
that and the other, only bits and pieces here and there,
but they ate tea just the same when it was set before
them. She said:
"'Tisn't any trouble, Miss. And you'd ought to be
hungry after your journey. If there's anything you want,
there's a bell right handy if you'll just ring it."
Kay didn't feel hungry till she began to eat but the
splits were hot and delicious. She piled them high with
jam and cream and ate three before she realized what she
had done, following them up with a slab of saffron cake
and four cups of tea. It was strong and heartening and
when she'd drunk it she felt curiously elated and inspirited.
She went over to look out of the window. The cottage
stood four square to the cove and the window of her little
sitting-room overlooked a garden that reached almost to
the water's edge. Coming in to clear the table and seeing
her there Mrs. Poldreath told her that at spring tides the
sea actually washed the garden wall.
"We've had tides that've washed right over the wall
now and again," she said. "But not lately. Seems to me
as though we don't get seas like we used to. I can re-
member"
She told Kay, who knew nothing of it, stories of the
78
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
sea that can only be told by the fisher folk who wrest from
it their living, and more often than not, find in it their
death. Of a husband who'd gone out in a boat that had
never come back, of a son who'd followed him. Little
graphic simple stories, the more graphic because of their
utter simplicity.
"'Tis a great thing, the sea," Mrs. Poldreath said. "An'
'tis a healer too, Miss, for all 'tis so cruel and cold. You'd
be surprised to know what a lot, besides bodies, sea air
can cure."
Kay went out after tea. Hatless so that the wind ruffled
her hair, coatless because even in April the winds are
warm on the south coast of Cornwall. The rocks that lay
between her and the sea were gray and smooth, almost
polished in the hard glossiness of their surface, with here
and there boats hauled up by the windlass that faced the
sea, lying bottom upwards to the setting sun. On one of
the rocks a mongrel dog lay stretched at full length on the
warm stones, peacefully untroubled. He opened a wary
eye as Kay passed him, watched her for a moment, shut
it again. The tide was falling and where the water had
been, the rock shone wet and glistening. A small boy in
shorts and a khaki shirt sailed a boat in a pool watched
disinterestedly by a row of gulls perched precariously on
a jutting rock ten yards from his sleek little dark head.
Every now and then one of them soared into the air, called
with shrill urgency to its fellows, dropped back to its place
alongside them on the rock. A woman from one of the
cottages came out with a baby in her arms. Seeing Kay
she nodded a friendly typical greeting, laid the baby in its
pram at the door, went back inside the cottage. The whole
place was utterly still, uncannily silent save for the cry of
the gulls and the murmur of slack water against the rocks
it washed, full of curiously restful peace.
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS 79
Kay thought:
"I may resent the necessity for all this but it is what I
need just the same. Rest of mind, rest of body. A year
here"
She went to bed at half-past nine in a room soaked in
fresh air through the open window of which the sea
sang her to sleep with a lullaby no other music can equal.
Slept like a log, sodden, drenched in sleep, until at ten next
morning Mrs. Poldreath woke her with breakfast on a
tray.
"You'm tired," she said. She put the tray on the table
by Kay's side, flung wider open still a window that looked
out over a turquoise blue sea. "I thought you'd be better
having your sleep out. Will you be in to lunch or would
you like me to put you up a bit of something?”
Kay said:
"Oh, sandwiches please. Anything. I've got to get out.
There's a little cove over there beyond that point. I saw
it last night and I want to get there. Can I get there, Mrs.
Poldreath?"
"That'll be Trewidden," Mrs. Poldreath said. She went
to the window, stood watching the tide for a minute,
turned back to the door. "Tide's falling," she said. She
used, like the boy of the night before, as few words as
possible. "You can get over the sands by noontime if
you'd care for that way, Miss. It's easier than by the
cliffs."
Bareheaded, in pullover and a tweed skirt, her feet in
thick low-heeled shoes, Kay crossed the sands just
after noon. They were wet still from the water that had
only just left them, and beside her the waves murmured
languorously, as they slapped the beach. She thought:
"I feel different already. As though my body as well
80
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
as my brain had been spring cleaned." She thought again
hopefully: "A year of this”
It was hard walking on the wet sands. They sucked at
her feet and before she'd accomplished more than half
of the trip she'd planned for herself she found herself
breathless and panting. She was furious with herself be-
cause so easily she found herself tiring. She thought,
struggling on, clutching her packet of sandwiches, wrench-
ing doggedly at her feet as they sank deeper and deeper
into the soft wet sands: "I won't give up. If I do this
today it's a step in the right direction. Tomorrow it'll be
easy and if I get on-round those rocks and as far as that
little cove-I've an idea the struggle'll be worth while if
I can only hang on until I get there."
The last quarter of a mile nearly beat her. She was done
when at last she rounded the headland and found herself
in the cove for which she'd set out. Exhausted and utterly
spent, and there wasn't, after all, anything that appeared
to really justify the struggle. She told herself, dropping
on to the warm dry sand below the shelter of the rocks:
"I'm a fool to do this. I'm all in and I've got to get
home. And besides there's nothing here, after all. Nothing,
at least, that I couldn't find in Polpendor, and yet I felt
so sure that, if I could get here anyhow I'd find something
worth while. It only goes to prove what a fool one can be.
How utterly stupid and idiotic."
She woke with a start to the sound of voices. Sat up
pushing her hair out of her eyes, blinking stupidly in the
sun. She must have slept, lying out there in the sun, for
hours. Her watch said twenty-past three, and she'd had
no lunch. Just lain there soaked in the sun, and slept.
The voices belonged to a man and a small boy. There was
a boat pulled up on the beach and the man was stooping
over it doing something with ropes that lay in the bottom
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
81
and the small boy was fussing about with fishing gear. He
had a little rod, obviously of his own, and was taking it
to pieces with meticulous care. Opening her packet of
sandwiches, munching them hungrily, Kay could hear

though the man talked to the child not as so many grown-
ups think it necessary to talk to children but as to an
equal. Discussing the day's catch solemnly and gravely,
the possibilities of better luck tomorrow, the chances that
some one known as Minnie would have the foresight to
provide crab for tea.
"I'm hungry," the small boy said. He was perhaps
seven, possibly eight. A thin wiry child burnt almost black.
He wore a pair of khaki shorts clutched about his slim
little waist by a tattered belt, with no vest or shirt, so that
his back and chest were bare; his hair was black and lay
sleekly against a singularly beautiful little head; his eyes
veiled by lashes far too long to be those of a boy, lying
black and silky against the dusky brown of his hard little
cheeks. "We didn't get as much lunch as usual, did we?"
"Oh, about the same I suppose," the man said. He
stood upright so that Kay saw his face for the first time.
He was tall, exceptionally tall, and unlike the child, heavily
almost clumsily built, but with the same brown skin and
sleek dark hair lying close to his head. His hands were
full of coils of rope, his shirt was collarless and wide
open to show a strong brown throat, his feet, below a
pair of ancient flannel trousers, clad in rubber shoes that
if they had once been white were certainly white no longer.
He threw the coils of rope on to the sand, felt in the
pocket of his trousers, produced a disreputable pipe and
lit it. "Not that it makes much difference really. I'm
afraid we're a hungry pair, my lad."
"I like being hungry," the small boy said earnestly.

SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
"Minnie says there's times when she just don't know how
to get her victuals down and that must be pretty awful.
I never get enough really to put down. At least I never get
so much that I can't get it down do you, Father ?"
"Not often, thank God," the man said with equal

"Don't think I'd better risk it," the man said. His voice,

I'll smoke a pipe here and wait for you."
The child fled across the sand, his little naked body
glowing in the sun, plunged headlong into the blue water,
came up shaking the wet out of his eyes, and swam easily,
with the long strokes of a practiced swimmer towards the
tiny jutting headland, fifty yards to westward. The man,
smoking beside the boat, sat watching him. It was clear
that he was tremendously proud of the lithe, beautiful,
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
83
little body; that, between child and man, there existed
not merely the affection of father and son but a solid
unshakable bond of fellowship and companionship. And
when, five minutes later, the boy came out and stood rub-

"You're improving quite a bit. But there's still room.
You hold your head too high. And your over-arm stroke's

because on the whole you're shaping rather well and I'd
like you to be really good. Are you dry? All right then,
slip on your shorts and we'll go home."
They went off together, hung about with baskets and
towels, bundles and fishing-tackle, the man walking with
a curious loping stride, the child trotting beside him in
an obvious effort to keep abreast.
Kay was sorry to see them go. There was something
oddly attractive about the pair so that, following on their
departure, she was conscious suddenly, of intense weari-
ness and of a sense of bitter rebellion against a fate that,
snatching from her everything that made life worth liv-
ing, could leave her stranded and alone. Without friend-
ship, without companionship, without apparently even the
ability or desire to work.
Dragging weary feet across the cliffs to Polpendor, be-
cause a rising tide precluded return by the sands, her
thoughts slid longingly to London and the life she'd left,
to the men and women who'd gone so far towards the
making of one's days so breath-takingly exciting; to Tony,
unselfish, generous, so dear and thoughtful always; last
of all, against her will, to Richard Veryan. She hadn't,
of course, been in love with him. Not as she'd hoped one
day she might fall in love but she'd liked him so much
84
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
that had he asked her she knew that quite definitely she
would have married him. That at the back of her mind
had lain almost ever since their meeting, the decision to
ultimately marry him. At the Sanatorium she'd thought
of him quite a lot, certain of his loyalty despite both the
rumors that reached her, and his absence. It was one
thing, she'd told herself lying there in her lonely little
garden hut, for a man to be in love with a lovely popular
star, and another to be equally in love with a dismal wreck

Veryan there was every excuse why he shouldn't have
come to the Sanatorium to see her. He wouldn't, for one
thing, want to see her looking ill and a wreck. To him
she was a beautiful woman and it stood to reason that he
would wish to think of her as such so that, finding her at
Archie Campion's party lovely as ever, beautifully dressed
by Simonie instead of draped in a shapeless bed-jacket,
the picture of health once again, she'd felt so sure of him.
Admittedly he hadn't telephoned her even on receipt of
her message, but that didn't mean anything. Servants were
terribly careless about the delivery of telephone messages
and he probably didn't even know she'd rung him up at
all. She knew now, too, that the reason she'd been so
fussy about her frock for the party had been because of
Richard. Because she'd been quite sure he would be at the

looking as attractive and as desirable as he'd grown accus-

had dropped out of her world. If once he saw her again
as he remembered her he'd forget that other glimpse of
her, hollow-eyed, hollow-cheeked, coughing out her very

seeing in her only the woman he loved and desired. And
then, after all, it had been no use. She'd taken into con-
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
85
sideration looks but she'd completely left out of her

count for anything at all. She'd been so sure that desire
would be the predominating factor with which she'd have
to reckon because, in the lives of nearly all the men and
women who made up her set, desire was the only factor
to be taken into consideration at all. If one wanted a thing
or a woman one took it or her without further thought,
and certainly without looking ahead to see where the tak-

always appeared to belong to the set that took no thought
to the morrow, fundamentally he didn't, as events proved,
belong to it at all. Because to him desire meant less than
his duty to his family and the necessity for insuring
healthy children.
At the back of her mind she had meant always to marry
Richard when she'd sucked quite dry the orange of free-
dom and enjoyment and having a good time. And now
Richard belonged, with everything else that made life
bearable, to the dead and buried past so that there was
nothing left. Nothing at all. Except years of loneliness
and struggle without anything at all to make either one
or the other worth while.
Mrs. Poldreath, bringing in tea, said:
"You've got yourself tired out, Miss, and I'm sorry for

a lot of us goes to hear him. 'Tis a treat to hear music

SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
Mrs. Poldreath. That is if you don't mind. Perhaps you'll

Drinking her tea in her little sitting-room she thought

her. "Where's the best place to learn d'you think? In my

They spent an enthralling hour together. There was,
apparently, nothing about boats the small boy didn't know.
He said superiorly :

father'll do without him."
"Hasn't he got a mother?" Kay said, and Mrs. Pold-
reath shook her head.
"His mother's a mystery, Miss. We don't rightly know
what 'tis, but she's ill somewhere. Mr. Cresswell don't talk
much. He's lived here now for six years, but no one knows
rightly what 'tis that's wrong."
"Cresswell?" Kay said. "Isn't that the man who you
said played the organ? At the church? Is Mr. Cresswell
Michael's father?"
"That's it, Miss. And a fine gentleman he is too. Not
one for talking much or making friends easy, but a fine
gentleman. I'm sorry you're too tired to come along and
hear him play, Miss. He's a rare hand with the organ."
"I'm not as tired now," Kay said. She thought: "Here's
some one else in trouble besides myself. Perhaps if I could
get to know him and talk to him I might learn not to be
so sorry for myself. If I knew that some one else was
hoeing a row as rough as mine" She sat down to the
table for supper. "I think I'll come after all, Mrs. Pold-
reath, if you'll take me."
There were perhaps a dozen or fifteen people in the
church when they went in. The choir practice was over
and a group of boys and girls were coming out as they
entered, with hymn books under their arms and humming
under their breath, and beside the organ a cassocked priest
stood talking to the man Kay had seen at Trewidden Cove
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
91
earlier in the day. An oil lamp burned over their heads and
another, small and red, hung before the high altar but the
body of the church was in darkness. A faint buzz of con-
versation greeted them as they went in, and Mrs. Poldreath
put a hand on Kay's arm.
"You come with me, Miss. It's dark, but I know my
way all right. There isn't any steps to fall over."
She spoke, Kay noticed, without troubling to lower her
voice, and over by the organ the priest and the man on the
bench were talking quite naturally. She thought:
"It's funny, but there's a sort of friendly feeling about
this place. As though one were welcome here. It's years
since I've been inside a church at all but I can't ever re-
member, even then, feeling like this about it." She said to
Mrs. Poldreath, following her into a pew: "You didn't
tell me you were taking me to a Roman Church."
"No more am I," Mrs. Poldreath said. "This isn't no
Roman Church, Miss. Folks as don't understand our ways
have said things about us being Papists an' goin' to Rome
an' a whole lot of silly rubbish but we aren't Romans.
You'll hear a lot o' things about us 'fore you've been in
Polpendor long I reckon, but you'll go a long way 'fore
you find a better man than our vicar, an' that's a fact.
That's him. Over by the organ. He's a fine gentleman is
Father Lawrence, Miss."
By turning a little in her pew Kay could see quite clearly
the two men by the organ. The organist, huge, brown,
broad-shouldered, the priest beside him, tall but thin al-
most to emaciation, the deep-set eyes of the æsthetic set in
a face that held all the peaceful serenity of a mediæval
saint. Later Kay was to learn a good deal of the man of
whom both churchman and dissenter, laity and priesthood,
saint and sinner for miles around spoke affectionately as
Father Lawrence. Of twenty years spent in a life of com-
92
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
plete unselfishness and utter self-denial in a tiny country
parish; of courage that enabled him to uphold his convic-
tions in the face of almost insuperable odds; of a faith and
devotion to his beliefs and to his Church almost childlike
in the serene certainty of their rightness. But at the time
her attention was held chiefly by the other man, who, sit-
ting astride the organ bench, was discussing Easter music,
who was Michael's father and who, together with herself,
had the cupboard of his life occupied by a skeleton of some
sort or another.
And undoubtedly he could play the organ. Knowing
nothing of music save that she liked the sounds made by
instruments beautifully played or by voices exquisitely pro-
duced Kay knew unhesitatingly that this man was no coun-
try organist. No amateur at the game. He played simple
things, but he played them with a touch of hands and feet,
a complete and utter sureness of confidence in himself that
left no doubt either as to his training or as to his experi-
ence. And, playing like that, able, Kay told herself, a little
bewildered, to play like that, he chose, of his own free will
apparently, to live in a place like Polpendor. To play in a
tiny village church on Sundays, to give pleasure once a
week to a cluster of simple fisher folk, to spend his life
where the appreciation that was his due could never come
his way, and living there, not because, obviously, since one
saw health blazing in capitals all over him, doctors had
given him the option of choosing between death and a
life in a sleepy fishing village, but because he liked it. Be-
cause he found pleasure and happiness there. Because, ob-
viously too, he preferred it to the life that, with his gifts,
could undoubtedly be his if he wanted it, and was, more-
over, happy in it.
Mrs. Poldreath went over to speak to him while he was
shutting the organ for the night. Kay, feeling her way to-
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
93
wards the door in the dim light cast by the solitary lamp,
heard only the murmur of her voice, but the answer came
as clear as a bell.
"You're letting sentiment run away with you, Mrs.
Poldreath. I've no doubt she's a quite estimable young
woman and immensely talented, but you ought to know me
well enough by this time to know young women don't in-
terest me. Even beautiful ones. I'm afraid you can't get
me interested in young women, Mrs. Poldreath."
Kay was glad of the darkness that hid her scarlet face.
He hadn't meant her to hear, of course. Or, had he? She
was furious with Mrs. Poldreath, with this supremely
self-centered egotistical person, with herself in that the
indifference to herself of a perfect stranger could so des-
perately hurt her self-esteem and her pride.
Mrs. Poldreath was still talking to the man at the organ.
Earnestly trying, no doubt, to persuade him. Kay, almost
in the porch, could see his cynical, tolerant smile, a little
amused and quite definitely firm. Mrs. Poldreath meant
well of course. One couldn't, feeling as sure of that as one
did, tell her that one would sooner die than ask for friend-
ship from so odious a creature, and that, as Kay Douglas,
one didn't need, in any case, to ask for friendship at all.
That one picked and chose one's own friends from a crowd
of applicants falling over each other in their anxiety to be
noticed; that one was sought rather than did the seeking,
because she hadn't meant to hurt one. Or to bring back so
vividly and cruelly to one's memory the procession of
events that had culminated in one's present loneliness and
isolation, so that out of the kindness of a friendly heart
she had tried to find for her lodger agreeable and suitable
companionship. Kay heard the man at the organ laugh and
was faintly inexplicably shocked by the sound. As a child
she'd always been sent to Sunday school and to church.
94
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
"Churchgoing” in the Balham side street had set one upon
a pedestal of definite respectability from which one looked
down a little pityingly upon such of one's associates as
had the misfortune to be "chapel," but church had never
been a place in which one laughed. Or in which one felt
anything save uncomfortable and stiff and on one's best
behavior, fearful of criticism from "teacher" and the sub-
sequent slap across the side of the head that invariably fol-
lowed upon the heels of reported misdemeanors. Along
with her knowledge of Father Lawrence, Kay was to
learn in the months that followed her first appearance in
the little church of St. Gabriel, that to the majority of
people the little granite church was home. Holy ground
certainly, since the red lamp burning over the altar pro-
claimed the Perpetual Presence, but never a place associ-
ated in their minds with party manners or boredom or dis-
comfort. They came, those fisher folk, to the church as
they went into their own cheery firelit kitchens. Gladly,
eagerly, knowing that, inside its sheltering walls there was
to be found rest of body as well as peace of mind, that
there before the tabernacle that held a Presence as real to
them all as the church that contained It, they could lay
down the burdens of the day, assured that if earthly assist-
ance were not forthcoming with which to ease the load at
least they might find there the peace that passes all under-
standing and the heavenly help that, if it cannot lighten the
physical burden, can so surely strengthen the shoulders
condemned to the carrying of it.
She was to learn that and a great deal beside during
the long lazy summer days she spent in Polpendor, but at
the time she heard the laugh it shocked her. It added, in
some quite inexplicable way, the coping-stone to the storm
of dislike rising in her soul for the man who sat at the
organ. Stigmatized him once and for all in her mind as a
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
95
person who was not only obsessed with a sense of his own
importance but was also so unaware of the ordinary de-
cencies of life that he didn't even know that to laugh out
loud in church was an act only one degree removed from
actual sacrilege.
Mrs. Poldreath, hurrying to catch her up and panting
a little, said innocently:
"I wanted Mr. Cresswell to come along and have a bit
of talk with you, Miss. He's a nice gentleman and your
sort. Clever an' that an' now you've made friends with
the little boy I thought it'd be nice for both of you. But
he's queer in his ways and he don't make friends easy. I
thought maybe he'd stretch a point seeing you're here and
lonely same as he is, but he wouldn't. He's a nice gentle-
man and I'm sorry he's took that tone, but once he makes
up his mind there's no shakin' him. I've tried it time and
again, and I suppose I'd ought to know by now as 'tisn't
no good. Still it's a pity just the same. Seem to me you an'
he could have been real good friends if only he'd been a
bit more sociable like."
Kay said:
"Oh that's all right, Mrs. Poldreath. It was kind of you
to think of it, but please don't bother. When I came down
here I didn't expect to find a lot of social life, so please
don't worry about me." She added because her self-esteem
was still badly ruffled: "Besides, I don't think Mr. Cress-
well and I could ever really be friends. I'm quite sure he
and I would never see alike about anything, so I expect it's
just as well really. But I've enjoyed hearing him play all
the same. The music's been lovely. I hope you'll take me
with you again some other time, Mrs. Poldreath."
VIII
KAY saw a lot of little Michael Cresswell during the
week that followed. His father he told her, entirely with-
out resentment, was busy with Easter music and couldn't
spare time to go out fishing much this week.
"But I don't miss him nearly so badly this year now
you're here as I did last year you know, Kay. It's fun,
isn't it, having people to do things with, and really you're
learning awfully quickly."
She learnt a great many things, too, that had nothing
to do with boats in the walks they took together over the
cliffs and along the sands; in the hours when she sat be-
side him watching while he sailed his boat; in the games
of pretense they played together when she was the Span-
ish Armada and he was Drake or Sir Richard Grenville
dying heroically and gallantly on the Revenge's gory little
deck. That, for instance, his mother was ill and in a hos-
pital at a place called Brockley, a long long way away.
That she'd been there years and years and years, so that
he couldn't even remember her at all or even if he'd ever
seen her. That his father had once been organist of a great
London church, but "after mother got ill and had to go
away into the hospital he didn't like it any more and so we
came to live here instead." That they lived in a cottage
"not in just a sitting-room and a bedroom like you do at
Mrs. Poldreath's but in a whole cottage with all our own
chairs and things you know, Kay," and were looked after
96
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
97
by one Minnie who came at eight in the morning and left
after supper at night. That they got breakfast themselves,
concocting marvelous dishes over a Cornish range in the
winter and over an oil stove in the summer. That he could
read and write and do lots of arithmetic and had to do two
hours' lessons every morning from nine till eleven. That
he could swim and dive and float, ride a pony, fish, climb
rocks, tie knots like a proper sailor, and make himself
generally useful. That he would, if he had any luck at all
and could pass his exams, go from his new prep. school
into the Navy and that, to his father's grief and dismay,
he couldn't sing a single note in tune and had no desire
whatever even to look at either an organ or a piano.
There was, Kay gathered, no lack of money. The child
had everything for which he could possibly ask; toys of
every type and description and all of them expensive. The
car his father drove was small, but it wasn't by any means
of a cheap variety and in addition to the boat Kay had

in the harbor at Penzance. Kay and Michael went into

"You'd like her," he said, and Kay saw in his eyes the

you when I'm at school." He added in his grave little voice,

SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
99
was stiff and frigid, as cold as drops of iced water. "I'm

She learnt on all sides that he really was busy with the
Easter music. Father Lawrence liked music. Lots of it.
Kay didn't understand it much because he called the Sun-

which was what she'd been accustomed to when she'd been
accustomed to church at all, but the music was lovely. She
could at least appreciate that part of it and apparently
nothing less than the best obtainable from the limited
sources at their disposal satisfied either priest or organist.
From her window Kay used to hear the organ going hour
after hour, and every evening there were the practices, so
that she saw quite a lot of Michael and he obviously liked
being with her. He said once :
"You're the first lady, except Minnie, I've ever known
really well. I don't think my father likes ladies much, so
I expect most of them aren't like you because he'd simply
have to like you if he knew you." He added with that un-
canny insight which is the prerogative of so many only
and sensitive children. "You're happier now than you were
when you first came, aren't you? D'you like living at Pol-
pendor, Kay?"
"Yes," Kay said. The answer was out before she'd
had time even to consider it, but surprisingly, when she
did stop to think of what she had said, she knew she had
spoken the truth. She was happy. Not, of course, as she'd
been happy in the mad glad days of those three vanished
years. That sort of happiness was over. Finished. Done
with. She'd never again be happy as she'd been then.
With London at her feet, with men at her beck and call,
with practically all that the world had to offer laid out
for her to choose from. She'd never know that sort of
happiness again, but while that was gone beyond all recall

SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
something had remained that had made life curiously
worth while all over again. She thought: "I suppose

haven't any other friends not to be friends themselves. I
told him that too."
"Yes?" Kay said. She was interested and at the same
time irritated because she could be at all interested in any

"I don't know," Michael said honestly. "He laughed but

he and pretty ladies hadn't anything in common. What
exactly does 'in common' mean, Kay?"
And then, all at once, Francis Cresswell and the pretty
lady found that they had a great deal in common. Three
days before Michael was due to go to school, with the
necessity for Easter music over at last so that he was free
once again to go out fishing and for walks, he and Michael

Geste and she was Major André crossing the desert to

"When once a landslide starts you never know where

The two of them were nearly at the top of the cliff now
and very soon they'd turn on to the narrow ledge. Michael
looked tiny against the gray face of the rock and even his
father had lost a good deal of his height and breadth. The
cliff was at its highest just there, which was why Michael
always chose that particular bit to climb.
"I'm going to be a real climber when I grow up," he'd
told her over and over again. "Not to climb just little
things, but big mountains like the Alps and perhaps even
Mount Everest. So you see, Kay, I've simply got to get
into practice."
Kay hadn't any head for heights so she'd always kept

well away from the edge on the climbing expeditions

She knew she was being silly to run shouting like that

ticed runner, an athlete in perfect training, she couldn't

the landslide had happened.
She kept on shouting wildly, hysterically, knowing it to
be useless but that to shout was all she could do.

ling."

IX
AFTERWARDS, looking back, Kay never knew
how she managed to reach him so soon. How, panting for
breath and sobbing with terror, she was able to get across
those jagged teeth, but she did it-somehow. As uncon-
scious of the pain that wrenched at her over-taxed, breath-
less lungs as of her bleeding hands, as she grabbed at
anything that would help her to find a foothold over the
slippery rocks. And then, turning the corner that had hid-
den him from her sight as she ran, she gave a little gasping
cry of relief because he wasn't on the rocks. By some mir-
acle, by what could be nothing less than a miracle, the boy
had fallen clear of those hideous gaping jaws and lay on
a tiny patch of sand-terribly still, with a scarlet stream
oozing from a gash just above his temple, where falling,
his head must have struck something, but at least he wasn't
on the rocks. If he'd fallen on those jagged teeth-
Kay thought, driving herself on to reach him: "I want
to be sick and I mustn't. I've got to keep my head," and
then at last found herself beside him on the little patch of
hot, golden sand, kneeling beside him because she daren't
even attempt to pick him up, struggling desperately to
stanch the blood that poured from his head with a hand-
kerchief soaked in one of the pools near by.
She heard Cresswell coming; his scrambling, urgent
progress over the slippery rocks; spoke to him without
looking up.
105
106
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
"He's alive. He fell clear of the rocks. I didn't try to
lift him. I was afraid. It was the landslide-I tried to
warn him and I couldn't. What shall I do to help. Please
let me help."
"Go back to the village for a stretcher," Cresswell said.
He was kneeling, now, beside the little still figure, his face
gray and grown suddenly old and lined. "Tell them to
bring anything. We've got to carry him flat. Quite flat.
And then go to the post office and telephone Penzance for
Archer, and London for Richards. I don't know the num-
ber. He lives in Devonshire Place." He was absolutely
calm and collected despite the gray livid face and terror-
drenched eyes, and he took her presence there, as well as
her offer for help, utterly for granted. "Trelawney's the
best man in the village. You can trust him, only-tell him
to be quick."
She turned without a word, running, despite the pain
in her chest, back the way she'd come. Repeating, as she
ran, over and over again the names of the two doctors.
"Archer in Penzance. Richards in London. In Devon-
shire Place. Archer. Richards." Mixing them with a des-
perate appeal to Some One, to Any One, who might per-
haps be listening and able to help. "Oh, don't let him
die. He mustn't die. Not Michael. Don't let him die. He's
so little. Richards in Devonshire Place. ... In Devonshire
Place.... Richards.... And Archer in Penzance...."
Young George Curnow, mending a net on the cove at
Polpendor, saw her round the corner, stared for a minute,
sensed trouble and ran sure-footed as a goat to meet her.
"What's wrong, Miss?" He caught her arm, steadying
her. "You're all out o' breath. Steady, Miss."
"Michael," Kay said. She could scarcely speak for the

Trelawney... and a stretcher. I'm all right. It's Michael

He turned in a flash, skipping like the goat of which

Big John Trelawney had found a gate, wrenched it,

a cliff path carrying a stretcher wouldn't be easy. They

hard dry sand; were abreast the jutting rocks when Kay
reached the post office.
Mrs. Dodson had heard already. Not everything but

"Where's the reason in this?" Kay said. She sat with
her chin in her hands staring out through the window.

115
lights of the fishing fleet flickered and danced. Close by in

before poor human devils like myself can see in Him any-

It was characteristic of the priest that he made no effort
to prove his case. Later Kay was to know that Lawrence
Bradley never argued. Never made any attempt to justify
a religion that to him was meat and drink, or a faith as

"You're feeling sore," he said quietly. "And the good
God knows it's only natural, my dear chap. But eat your
supper, Cresswell, and if I'm wanted-you know where
you can find me."

sand, thousand times worse. I've at least had something

Engrossed in resentment for the child she forgot, for

"When I see that baby I get so sick with myself I could

They went over and spent an hour with Michael. Tony
said:
"Michael, listen, have you ever watched Kay trying to
dive? Look while I show you."
He gave a ridiculous burlesque of Kay, who couldn't
dive, plunging flatly into deep water, floundering wildly,
coming up panting and blowing, followed it with a care-
fully expurgated edition of some of the sketches taken
from a recent revue, and left the child helpless with laugh-
ter.
"Kay, bring him again. Please tell him to come again.
He's funnier than anything I've ever seen. Do bring him
again."
Tony, an arm flung round Kay's shoulders, said:
"I seem cut out by Providence to amuse and encourage
the sick. D'you suppose I've missed my calling, Kay?
What about the circus for a change?"
"You're sweet," Kay said. She saw Francis Cresswell
crossing the stones ahead of them, saw his eyes rest on
Tony's encircling arm, the cynical twist that spoilt his
mouth as he bowed to her in passing, and stiffened un-
consciously. She said: "That's his father. Michael's, I
mean. We hate each other like the plague."
"Quite a satisfactory beginning," Tony said and grinned
at her. "If you read the works of lady novelists you'll
realize that's exactly as it should be. Invalid child, strong
silent father, invisible mother, charming young actress at
a loose end. All the ingredients for a really excellent ro-
mance, Kay, my dear. Staged in a highly romantic setting
such as this village, to appropriate music, it ought to go
uncommonly well."
"Oh," Kay said. "You're such a fool, Tony. If you
124
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
could see us together and hear us. We're barely civil to
each other. He can't tolerate me."
"Hiding a secret passion under a cloak of indifference,"
Tony said, and grinned again irrepressibly. "But don't
forget I've warned you, Kay. In our profession we don't
have much chance to cultivate the strong silent type, so
that you're doubly vulnerable. I'd hate to see you swept off
your feet fighting a passion you were unable to control,
darling."
There didn't, however, seem much chance of Tony's
nicely-planned little Romance materializing. In the days
and weeks that made up the long lazy Cornish summer
Kay and Francis Cresswell met when they had to meet,
and by mutual consent avoided one another whenever
possible. The trained hospital nurse had gone now. There
was nothing to be done for Michael any more, save amuse
him and feed him and wash him, and a jolly Irish widow
who'd had children of her own and knew how to handle
them, who was good-tempered and amusing and ready to
play games with him when neither Kay nor his father
were available, had taken the place of starched officialdom
in the little cottage at the far end of the cove. Michael knew
the truth now. They'd kept him in ignorance for nearly
four months, and then at last Father Lawrence had told
him. What happened no one knew. Neither Michael nor
Father Lawrence ever threw light on to what passed in
the two hours the priest and the child spent together in
Michael's room; but that night, long after the light was
out in the little room that overlooked the sea, when a
deftly administered sleeping draught had flung a kindly
curtain over an agonized childish mind, Kay, who'd seen
Father Lawrence leave and knew what he'd been doing,
went down in the soft summer darkness to sit on the
beach and try to work out for herself the whys and where-
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
125
fores of the puzzle life spread out in front of her. She sat
there for perhaps an hour. The rocks were empty now.
Deserted by the last of the summer visitors who filled the
cove all through the hot August days. There was nothing
that they could see to be gained by spending a holiday
evening sitting on rocks in the darkness staring out to sea
while there were pictures to be had in Penzance, drinks at
the inn a mile farther inland, bridge to be played by the
light of paraffin lamps that lent to even a game of cards
the glamor of mysticism and romance. So that the beach
was empty. Far out to sea a light at the masthead of a pass-
ing vessel winked now and then, slid from east to west,
vanished round the headland that hid Polpendor from
Trewidden on the opposite side, and went its silent, pur-
poseful way. Once or twice a gull screamed shrilly, eerily,
settled again into the shadows; the cracked clock on the
little granite church struck eleven, lights in the windows

darkness. All the lights, that is, save in the cottage next
to Mrs. Poldreath's. Sally Tregenna was having her baby
in that cottage. She'd come in while Kay was having sup-
per to fetch Mrs. Poldreath, and Kay had heard her talk-
ing.
"'Tis all right, but I'd be glad if you'd come in soon.
The pains is settin' in proper now. I don't think it'll be so
long this time."
Behind that lighted window a woman's soul faced
agony and death to bring a son into the world; behind an-
other darkened window a child of eight lay, drug-sodden,
so that he might sleep, and in sleep forget the torture of
wakefulness; out on the beach a girl whose hand had
grasped for a fraction of time the flaming torch of success
sat asking herself and the silence questions to which no
human being has yet been able to supply the answer. And

SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
away beyond the headland a big man, hatless, coatless, lay

Kay saw him rounding the headland just before mid-

washed out of existence as though it had never been, be-

"He mustn't see me. I can't trust myself. If I said a

he'll go by without even seeing me. In the shadows like
this if I don't move or breathe”
But, by some trick of the light he saw her, stood still for

"I've been trying to forget his eyes and I can't. I think

127
ing. I've found that out for myself. If you stay here for

But before the reply came something happened that
altered everything. It came when Michael had been lying
in his straight little bed for nearly ten months; when Kay's
term of exile was all but over; when the fogs and misting
rain and cold west winds of a Cornish winter were being
replaced once again by sunshine and the glow of flowering
bulbs, and when Kay went into Penzance to find a book
and a new jig-saw puzzle for Michael.
It was difficult in these days to find anything that really
amused him; still more difficult to recognize in the chilc
who lay listless and inert on the little straight couch unde
the window, any resemblance to the child who'd spent hi.
whole life out of doors; who'd sailed his boat in the pool.
amongst the rocks, fished and swum and climbed; who'

SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
129
truth, lying in bed, being waited on hand and foot, being

Michael could glue it, himself, into place. It was all beau-

putting it all together and painting the name on its stern.
Robert Willis brought a model farm. Only a rough model
certainly, made on a square slab of painted wood with
animals from Woolworths, but Michael had played with
it for days inventing marvelous games. Mrs. Poldreath

SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
otherwise must have hung like leaden chains about the

But while it had all been rather fun at first it didn't stay
fun. One got tired of bed, even of bed surrounded with
marvelous toys and interested visitors, after one had been
there for simply months and months, so that one began to
ask questions and to be puzzled a little because every one
seemed so vague. To avoid giving one straight answers
to the straight questions of "when" and "how much
longer," until at last Father Lawrence had come in for tea
one day and instead of being vague and evasive when
asked a perfectly straightforward question, had told one,
very gently, the truth, and killed, in one single moment, all
the fun the world had ever held.
Life after that day took on a different aspect altogether.
One couldn't get really interested in anything any more.
People were kind but nothing was the same. Even Father
wasn't quite the same in these days. He looked miserable
and unhappy however hard he tried not to show it and to
be gay and bright and ready to play any game under the
sun, but it wasn't the same. Nothing was the same now.
Nothing, Michael told himself, with the tragic despair of
childhood, would ever be the same any more. Now that
for always and always and always he'd have to stay,
chained to the narrow straight little bed and never run
and swim and climb again as long as he lived.
They carried him down on to the beach one day, but
only once. He'd liked it at first until a little boy of about
his own age, a visitor for the summer, had run past him,
had plunged into the water and easily, beautifully, had
swum out to Michael's own rock. Then he'd turned ago-

nized eyes on his father and on the fat cheery Irishwoman

"I don't want to stay here, Father. Take me back.

'NOW, let's look at you," Brian Onslow said. He and

"Didn't some one tell me I've been hearing things or

Briefly, unemotionally Kay told him.

bill of health a month ago and I've stayed here, saying
nothing even to Tony, because I'm afraid."
Brian Onslow said blankly:
"Afraid? Of what in God's name? What the devil has
a girl in your shoes to be afraid of?"
She told him that too. -Quite quietly and evenly.

sorry. Of course it can't matter to you why I'm going, but
I thought you see some one's got to tell Michael. He's
fond of me, bless him, and he'll have to know. I thought
if I told you”
"Yes," Francis Cresswell said. His voice was even and
flat and quite devoid of expression. "I'll tell him. He'll be
upset of course, but that can't be helped. It had to happen
sooner or later." He added formally: "I hope you'll let
me congratulate you on your approaching come-back, Miss
Douglas. And your recovery."
Kay, hurrying back to be ready for Onslow, found her-
self thinking of Cresswell. Almost against her will.
"He looks so tired and old. And beaten. As though
there were nothing worth while anywhere. If only there
were something one could do to make things easier." Then
she remembered his chilly formalities, his abrupt greetings
and partings, and catching sight of Onslow's approaching
car dismissed him from her thoughts.
The play was good. Better than good. It gripped one's
attention, held it breathless till the last fall of the final
curtain. The study of an ideal marriage confronted at the
end of fifteen years with a proved charge of bigamy; the
wife's reactions; her plans; her superb unswerving de-
votion to an ideal that carried her triumphant through
every phase of disillusionment and heartbreak. Leaning
back in her chair listening while Brian Onslow read it,
Kay could see it all; could visualize every scene, every
detail.
With the growing inferiority complex of the last year
swept aside, forgotten, ignored, she knew that what
Onslaw had said was true. This was her play, her part.
It might have been written for her. She thought:
140
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
"I can do it. I can play this on my head. That woman-
she's doing all the things I'd do. Crazy, unbelievable, im-
possible things, but things that are right. That one knows,
despite all their craziness, are just the things that she
would do. If he means what he says, that he wants me
Oh God, God, what a chance I've got."
Shutting up the covers of the script Brian Onslow said:
"Well, what about it, Kay?" His voice was smug, full

They sat up till long past midnight, thrashing things

Miss Douglas," and Kay laughed.
"Tired?" she said. "I'll never be tired again as long as
I live. If once I play this”
It was nearly one when Brian Onslow finally left, and
then things were practically settled.
"We'll go back together on Monday, Kay. In the car.

the whole thing fixed up. I'll call for you at nine o'clock

SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
Michael" she broke off, flushing. "I'm boring you to

It was horrible to ignore his pleading, to turn one's

Traveling up with Brian Onslow in the car; tearing Lon-

"I'd no business to come away. He needs me and he's

SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
143
this up, when it's my chance? A chance that comes only

Inside the little bedroom everything was terribly quiet

thing else in the room too, to which one daren't give a
name and which even while one couldn't see it or hear it
or touch it one knew was there. Waiting in utter silence
and stillness, an invisible terrifying Presence that, with
Time and Eternity as the toys to be played with at will,
could afford to bide its time and spare to those who loved
him a moment longer with the child It was waiting to
take away with It.
Francis Cresswell said:

voice of which critics had raved-sounded oddly clear in

It seemed to the three who stood there watching an
Eternity before the child's eyes opened. As though the

hope they saw the lids quiver.
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
155
"Michael, please!" Kay said again, and at last his eyes
opened. His hand was so nearly in that of the waiting
Presence that already he was almost out of touch with
the things of the world that had been his, and for a
moment, full of the wonders of another newer world,
they failed to recognize even the one person for whom
he had longed so desperately in the three weeks since she'd
left him. And then, watching him, they saw the shadows
of the future slide away to give place to recognition of
the present; saw the light that crept into their tired, ex-
hausted depths, the weak heart-breaking little smile that
flickered for a barely percepible second round the tired
childish mouth, and heard his voice. Scarcely audible, so
weak that it was hardly more than a breath and they had
to strain every muscle even to hear what he said.
But they all heard it. Even, it seemed, the Presence
heard it too, because afterwards all of those who listened
and watched so closely knew that, at that moment, some-
thing had moved a step further from Michael's bed so
that the air became curiously less heavy, less pregnant with
danger, less terrifyingly full of fear and that queer in-
definable consciousness of fear.
"Oh, Kay!" Michael said. "I thought you were never
coming. I thought you'd forgotten me. Don't ever go
away any more, will you, Kay?"
XV
FRANCIS CRESSWELL, facing Kay in the
little sitting-room of Mrs. Poldreath's cottage, said:
"There's nothing I can say-nothing I can do you
saved his life, and beyond saying I'm grateful I can do
nothing. Between us we've wrecked your career and ruined
your future, and there's nothing we can do to make it up
to you apart from expressing gratitude, which at its best
is cold comfort. I saw the paper this morning, so that I
know exactly what we've done, you see."
Kay had seen the paper too. Not one, but many. She'd
been into Penzance on the bus and bought them all, and
then for two hours she'd sat on the rocks staring out to
sea and realizing what she'd done. There'd been telegrams
too. For the three days that followed her arrival they'd
came in a steady stream. From Richways-one after an-
other-from Brian Onslow-from Tony. They'd been
polite at first-that, of course, had been the result of
Tony's silver tongue-then persuasive, then peremptory,
and then they'd been followed by telephone calls to the
post office, so that Mrs. Dodson's little grandson had spent
quite a considerable portion of his day running across to
the cottage with a message that "Miss Douglas was
wanted, please, on the 'phone." She'd ignored the telegrams
after the first one from Richways. There was no object in
replying to them since there was nothing she could say
and she knew, of course, exactly what she was doing.
156
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
157
She'd sent messages back in reply to the telephone calls
too that she couldn't possibly get across to the post office,
that things were just the same; that they must replace her
if necessary; that she couldn't leave. Afraid to go herself,
afraid to listen to the dear familiar voices lest temptation
should get the upper hand of resolution; lest the longing
to go back to everything she most desired should let her
for one moment forget Michael and his need of her. And
on the evening of the third day, when telegrams and tele-
phone calls had failed, Brian Onslow and Tony had raced
west on the Riviera to try what personal persuasion could
do. Kay had met them in Penzance. They'd had dinner at
the Queen's and for three hours they'd argued, wrangled,
coaxed, cursed, persuaded, implored.
Tony said:
"You're mad, Kay. I could understand your coming
down in the first place, but this is crazy. You're blind as
well as mad, or you'd see exactly what you're heading for.
Richways is livid. He swears he'll see you anywhere before
he'll look at you for another show unless you're back in
town in the morning. He's put off rehearsals, delayed
production-my God-there's a limit, you know, Kay,
and he's about reached it. He's been pretty damned decent
too, taking everything into consideration."
"Listen," Brian Onslow said. He leant forward,
banging a huge fist on the table. "We've had about enough
of this. Are you coming or aren't you, Kay? This is my
show and I'm damned if I see it ditched because a fool
woman doesn't know on which side her bread's buttered.
Are you coming back with us on the train tonight, or
aren't you? If you are, all well and good. If you're not,
I'll tell Richways to go right ahead and put that Dawson
woman in. She's crazy to do it anyway, so there'll be no
158
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
trouble there. The question is, are you coming or aren't
you?"
"I can't," Kay said stubbornly. "I've told you. Michael's
alive but that's all. If I leave him-oh!"-she said-
"don't you see? It's something I've got to do. I couldn't
look myself in the face again if I failed him. He's fond
of me. He loves me...."
"I love you," Tony said. "If I didn't d'you suppose I'd
come chasing across the country to try and make you see
reason? Kay, listen"
"Oh!" Kay said. "What's the use. We just go round
and round like squirrels in a cage. And get nowhere. D'you
think I don't know what I'm doing or that I don't want
to come with you? If you don't you're as crazy as you
think I am. I tell you, I've got to stay here. If I didn't
-surely you, at least, Tony, can understand that if I
didn't stay I could never look myself in the face again.”
In the end, seeing that further argument was useless,
they left her to catch the night train back to London, and
Kay went home in a bus that jostled and shook till she
wanted to scream, and in a drizzling mist that did more
to drive home to her the utter idiocy of the step she'd
taken than any argument either Tony or Brian Onslow
could have brought to bear on her.
And then today had brought the papers with criticisms
of the play's opening night; of the play itself; of the caste
in general; of Pamela Dawson and Tony in particular.
The critics had all been kind to it too. More than kind,
in fact, because while some of them were actually fulsome
in their praises all of them acknowledged without any
of the "buts" which are the prerogative of every critic
that "it was a magnificent piece of work." And practically
all of them applauded Pamela Dawson. Of Tony they
said, as so often they'd said before: "Mr. Seabright gave,
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
159
as usual, the interpretation one has learnt to expect from
this brilliant young actor." But it was Pamela Dawson's
low white forehead that really bore the load of their laurel
wreaths. They worded their criticisms differently, of
course, but shorn of their journalese, boiled down to the
dry bones of facts, they all meant the same thing.
"We have seen Miss Pamela Dawson on several pre-
vious occasions, but never before has this gifted actress,”
etc. etc. etc. John Adams of the Daily Review, Herbert
Mason of the Mercury, Stephen Warilow of the Morning
Noticeboard, they were all there. All saying the same thing.
Wording it differently but all meaning the same. With,
inset, photographs of Pamela Dawson herself. Demurely,
consciously beautiful, holding a copy of the script and with
her eyes on a far-away horizon; with a slim white hand
through the arm of a glowing young playwright; hand in
hand with Tony, smiling at him over a veritable Covent
Garden of what the papers called "floral tributes"; leaving
the stage door with Richways, almost mobbed by the
eager, admiring crowd that didn't mind how long it waited
so that it could go home and say it had seen her at close
range, touched her as she swept on her regal, triumphant
way, waylaid her for signatures in cherished albums.
Sitting there on the lonely little beach, Kay saw it all.
The shoes Pamela Dawson was wearing now were her
cast-offs. But for her own folly and a crazy beckoning
conscience that wouldn't let her rest or be sensible and
practical, she'd have been wearing them still. The flowers,
the applause, the fulsome criticisms, the crowds waiting at
the stage door, would have been hers. She'd had them all
actually in her hand and for a whim in a moment of
crazy insensate reasonless folly-she'd flung them all
aside, stepped out of shoes that had fitted her so beauti-
fully, leaving them for this other girl to find, had to stand
160
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
by and watch while Pamela Dawson tried them on and
found them also a perfect fit.
And then coming back to Mrs. Poldreath's with all the
ghosts of what might have been gibbering at her as she
walked amongst them, she'd found Francis Cresswell
waiting for her.
"I've seen the papers, you see. And there is nothing I
can do to make it up to you. Nothing. Except give you my
thanks, which are so damned inadequate."
She thought, listening to him:
"If only he were always like this-human-part of the
ordinary world. Like this he's attractive terribly attrac-
tive-but as a rule talking to him is like talking to a
figurehead. If he were always like this we might even be
friends”
She began :
"If I've helped Michael" and he broke in across
her flat toneless voice:
"You've not only helped him. You've saved his life. I
tell you he'd have died if it hadn't been for you. He's
mad about you. I've never known him like this about any
one, but even so I'd no right-no right in the world to let
you do this. If things had even been different" He
caught himself up short, obviously uncomfortable and em-
barrassed. "Later on, we've got to talk things over and
see what can be done because you've given up so much,
but just now I don't say much. I'm a cross-grained
ill-conditioned devil for most of my time although I'll
explain that some day if you'll let me, but I would like
you to know this. Nothing I can ever say or do will be
able to prove my gratitude to you for what you've done
for me, Kay."
It wasn't until after he'd gone, until she was eating
her solitary lunch in the quiet little sitting-room, that she
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
161
remembered his use of her Christian name. And realized
that, because of it, some of the gloom that filled the whole
world had lifted a little, and that in the far far distance
there was a pale watery gleam of something that might
mean sunshine.
It was funny, remembering all she'd thought of Francis
Cresswell in the past thirteen months, to realize how
warmly comforting, how friendly, how like a beacon
shining through the fog, had been his simple unexpected
use of her Christian name.

IT was Archer's suggestion, made six weeks later when
the sunny days of summer were already beginning to push

Waterhen.
"There's no reason why he shouldn't go," he said.
"And why it shouldn't do him a world of good. It'll give

can't help a little human interest cropping up now and

Miss Douglas is a damned pretty woman. Ever noticed it?"
"Several times," Francis Cresswell said dryly. "What
d'you propose I should do about it?"
"God knows," Dr. Archer said piously. "And that's
the truth." He added: "You're not a bad-looking chap

SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
were free I'd probably marry again for Michael's sake.

It was quite apparent, however, that there was gossip.
Cresswell knew it well enough; Father Lawrence knew it;
Kay herself guessed it. And, after all, it was only natural.
Women, young and good looking, didn't as a rule bury
themselves in tiny villages unless there was some quite
adequate reason for their being there, and while Michael
provided a certain amount of ground, even Michael-in
the eyes of Polpendor-couldn't compensate a lovely
woman for giving up her career and spending the rest of
her days in a back-o'-beyond fishing village where no one
ever came either to see her or to admire her. And if there
were little or nothing on which they could actually lay a
defining finger they at least drew their own conclusions
and made the best of the little they did see.
That there was so little was apparently the fault of
neither participant in the drama. The old days of the queer
armed neutrality that had characterized their earlier ac-
quaintanceship had gone certainly. One couldn't, after that
scene in Michael's room, standing side by side, scarcely
daring to breathe while the Waiting Presence beside them
gradually released his hold on the child they both loved,
be strangers any more. They had even found grounds
of mutual friendship and companionship-books, music,
theaters-so that they'd actually got something other than
Michael to discuss when they met either outside or inside
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
165
his room, but no one-not even the most romantically-

Writing to Tony-still resentful and inclined to lecture

"They're all weaving a marvelous Romance about us
which is pathetic when one looks at facts. I like Francis
Cresswell immensely-we're actually friends these days-
but I can't possibly see myself in love with him, still less
he with me. I think life's treated him almost as roughly

sympathy and understanding, but he never says much.
And no one knowing us could possibly read anything ap-

I think I must have spent all my love in my job because
never in my life have I felt less desire for sentiment in
any shape and form than I do just now, although you
mustn't think I'm unhappy, because I'm not. On the con-

think I could bear it, and now I'm beginning to think that
probably this was really the sort of life I was intended to
lead, because I'm quite happy. I get up at half-past eight;
I eat three good meals a day; I go for long walks; I play
games with Michael; I knit jumpers for myself and am

166
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
didactic and bigoted; I go to church on Sunday evenings

It's queer, isn't it, to remember what I was, and then to
think of me as I am now, and yet know that I'm really
genuinely happy."
It was queer. Very queer. But it was true none the less.
Life in Polpendor contained no single ingredient of life in
London. One might be in another world altogether, a
world made up of different people, of different ideas, of
different outlooks. There was nothing in it that could be
compared even distantly with the life one had imagined
only a little over two years ago to be the only life worth
living, and yet, inexplicably, unbelievably, but quite truth-
fully, one was happy. One had health; one had, acting on
Tony's advice, invested all one's money in a tiny annuity,
so that one was at least safe from the carking fear of
what would happen when one's capital gave out; one had
Michael who, from one small inert body exuded enough
admiration to fill the stalls of a theater; one had peace
and one had friends, Father Lawrence and Francis Cress-
well who, imperceptibly, and when no one was there to put
finger on time or place had become "Francis," and who,
lonely too, took up, really, the greater part of the day
Michael left unoccupied.
Life in Polpendor, Kay told herself complacently, at
the end of May, wasn't the life she'd planned. It was dull
and circumscribed; it had neither parties nor applauding
crowds; it could produce neither footlights nor Bond
Street bouquets, nor coroneted luxurious saloon cars wait-
ing for one at stage doors nor young men clamoring for
even the slightest, slenderest favor. It was, for that matter,
the exact antithesis of everything she'd ever thought of or
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
167
planned and hoped for, and yet it was all very happy and
peaceful.
Struggling with the intricacies of the pullover for Fran-
cis, it was astonishing to think how peaceful and happy
it all was. And when in June the question of the cruise on
the Waterhen came up for discussion, and, in answer to
Michael's reiterated insistent demands, Francis said: "But
of course Kay's coming, Michael. We couldn't possibly go
without her, could we?" it really seemed that, after all,
there might be in life something that made it a great deal
more than just worth the struggle and worry of living.

IN the end they didn't go in the Waterhen after all, be-
cause she was too small.
Francis Cresswell, poring over charts and maps, mak-

"It seems to me we're going to be a fair crowd taking

first and he's always a bit of a trial on account of the
retinue he brings aboard. So you can be owner and be
as much of a nuisance as you like, and for retinue you can
have Mrs. Maloney, who'll take up enough room for two.
Then there'll be Kay, who's one of the owner's guests;
and there'll be Dr. Steele, who's going to be ship's doctor;
and there'll be the captain who, of course, is me; and
there'll be the crew. So that since the Waterhen seems
likely to prove a bit of a tight fit, what d'you think of this
for a tub?"
"Oh!" Michael said, and drew in his breath with a
little whistle of admiration. "Oh-Father-what a per-

let's have her."
"We've got her," Francis Cresswell said. "She's sitting
in Penzance Harbor at this moment and tomorrow we go

aboard her. If you get excited, Michael, you'll be left be-

They spent a delightful evening, and next morning was
tremendously exciting. Michael waked at five to superin-
tend, from his bed, his own packing. There were things
that, without personal supervision, would undoubtedly be
left behind and which were of vital importance. One could,
he explained to Mrs. Maloney, fish perfectly well even if
one did have to lie flat, so that fishing tackle must be on
the list of things to be included. And the yacht too. What
was to prevent a long string being tied to the yacht and
held in one's hand even in one's bed? She wasn't the type
of yacht that capsized or gave trouble of that sort, neces-
sitating immediate wadings through water in order to
rectify the damage, so that there was no reason-no rea-
son at all-why she shouldn't go, and why she shouldn't
be able to indulge in marvelous regattas with other less
imposing craft to be produced by Father and Kay and
Dr. Steele, who was younger than most doctors and knew
how to tell good stories and was exactly, one felt sure,
the type of person best suited to be a ship's doctor. And

"He's been goin' on like that ever since five this morn-

such an int'rest in everything an' wantin' to be goin' this

It was fun being on the yacht and tremendously luxu-
rious. Cresswell had thought of everything, so that there
was nothing lacking that could possibly make for com-
fort, and for Kay, at least, it had all the charm of complete
novelty. Michael said with the superiority of the confirmed
yachtsman towards one who knows none of the ropes:
"You'll find it funny at first, I expect-but you'll soon

about staircases on board a ship. I did think I'd taught
you enough about boats to know that much."
"I'd forgotten," Kay said apologetically. She'd come to
say good-night to him, and standing beside him where he
lay in his narrow little bed under an awning on the after-
deck, scenes towards which familiarity tended to deaden
admiration took on in the darkness a new charm and a
mystery that, in the daylight, had been foreign to them.
Penzance Harbor, for example. In the daytime it was just
a space that was sometimes covered by water and was
sometimes simply an expanse of repulsive mud on which
boats lay on their sides in attitudes of rakish abandon.
One couldn't possibly read Romance into Penzance Har-
bor in the light of day, but at night it was all quite differ-
ent. The lamps that fringed the town; riding lights that
flickered at the mast-heads of anchored vessels; head-
lamps that glowed in the bonnets of cars speeding up and
down the promenade, gave the place at night a different
outlook altogether. One could think of lots of things about
it. Things that were absorbing, romantic, enormously
thrilling. Of smugglers creeping alongside the quay; of
sailors from distant lands, boisterous and swaggering,
roistering along the narrow streets, winking at the girls
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
171
in doorways, looking for fun and taking it where they
found it; of quiet, patient-eyed women watching the fish-
ing fleet put to sea and wondering how many of the men
who manned the gayly-sailed little vessels would be spared
to come back again. It was easy to read almost anything
into the Harbor in the soft whispering darkness just as
one could read things into the mass of granite that, lying
to the eastward, towered skywards and kept guard over
the bay and was St. Michael's Mount. So that one saw

chink of glass as warriors drank to the health of their
lord, or toasted a woman's eyes in wine that glowed
red in the flickering light of the torches that surrounded
a table groaning beneath the luxuries it held. Standing
there beside Michael, listening while he talked, it was
almost startling to discover the lengths to which one's
imagination could wander; the stories it could invent; the
pictures with which without help of any kind apparently
from oneself it could fill one's brain. "I really am sorry,
Michael, but I won't forget again. And in any case I did
remember to call the kitchen the galley to-night, and you've

They went to Falmouth first of all. Francis Cresswell,
submitting plans to the owner, said:
"I suggest no definite itinerary, Michael. What d'you
think? Just to go wherever the fancy takes us, beginning
with Falmouth. There's nothing, d'you think, so really
exciting and absorbing as to set sail without having a
single idea as to where one's actually going."
So they began with Falmouth, and headed east, stop-
172 SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
ping overnight wherever they chanced to find themselves.
With Michael as a definitely autocratic owner; with
Francis Cresswell growing less taciturn, less silent, less
self-absorbed with the passing of each day as captain;
with Dr. Steele as physician-in-charge; with Mrs. Ma-
loney, who hated it all from start to finish and could be
seasick with no provocation whatever, but who was far too
fond of Michael to let him set sail without her; and with

fishing village to busy seaport town; from popular seaside
resorts to lonely coves where for population there were
only gulls and fish and here and there a flashing, darting
cormorant; till the grim rock-circled coast of Cornwall

till they crept past Portland Bill, where Mrs. Maloney
prayed to all her gods for speedy sudden death, and
where Michael shouted with joy when the yacht dropped
her nose into the sparkling green water and the spray
soaked the bed Steele and Cresswell had lashed to her
rail; till they left the coast of Dorset behind and slid
into the quiet blue restfulness of the Solent that, with its
physical restfulness, seemed to bring, too, peace of soul
and mind and a curious, unquestioning tranquillity of
spirit. That made one content with life as it came; en-
abled one to accept willingly what each day brought forth;
to find oneself satisfied with the truth of the saying that
"sufficient for the day is the evil thereof" and so oddly
inexplicably detached from the world beyond the yacht's
rails as to find a definite full stop set to the worries and
puzzles and questionings of the past two years.
It was on one of those long, lazy summer evenings
when Michael was asleep that Kay learnt, from Steele,
something of Cresswell's early life.
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
173
"He'd everything at his finger-tips," Steele said. They
