the harbor in which he planned to spend the night. "He
and I were at school together and he'd everything. Money,
ability, all the things he wanted. He was organ-mad. He'd
always been the same, and he was twenty-three when he
went to St. Augustine's. It was a big job for a chap his
age. St. Augustine's is one of those fashionable churches
where music plays a big part. There used to be recitals and
things every Sunday evening before service and people
came from miles just to hear him play. His people were as
proud as peacocks. Stiff-necked Victorians of the old
school, and while they thought that even being an organist
was bad form and infra dig. and a lot of tommy rot of
that sort, they might have swallowed his job if it hadn't
been for the people he made friends with in the doing of
it. I don't know how Cresswell's people ever had Cresswell
for an only child, because they'd nothing in common with
him anywhere, and they'd less in common with his friends,
and less still with the girl he married." He paused for a
minute in his story to ask a question. "He never told you
about his wife, did he?"
"No," Kay said. "I've often wondered. People talked-
hinted at things, but no one seemed to know anything
definite. Once Francis said he'd tell me or at least that's
what I thought he meant and then there is something,
isn't there?"

"There's a lot really, but, shorn of details, it needn't take
long. She was a dancer in a music hall, and lovely. She'd

SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
that queer unreal loveliness that makes a man feel a

since but the scandal broke him. The vicar was tremen-

all that, but that scene smashed Cresswell altogether. I

suppose, at the bottom of his make-up, there was a good

Later-with the anchor dropped, without even the hum

Steele said:

"No, thanks," Kay said, and Cresswell shook his head.
"Not for me yet," he said, and when Steele went below,
stood for a second beside the rail, then came over and

"I heard him talking," he said. He lay back in his chair,
his long legs propped against the cross-bar of the rail, his
arms folded and his eyes staring out into the darkness. It
was after eleven and behind them lights ashore were be-
ginning one by one to go out. Somewhere in the shadows
of a yacht anchored fifty yards distant a boy was singing
to the accompaniment of a twanging ukulele, the silly
sentimental twaddle of the words punctuated every now
and then by a girl's laughter and a mocking, teasing voice.
On the Corisande everything was quiet now. Mrs. Ma-
loney, abandoning weeks ago her dual rôle of chaperon,
had long since gone to bed. In sleep one forgot the horrors
of the sea; the miseries of unsteady little yachts that
plunged and rolled and leapt up and down in fathomless
green water, and the longer one spent in one's bed, the
earlier one went to it, the more comfort one got out of a
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS 177
life that for nearly two months had been entirely devoid
of real comfort of any sort whatever. Forward in the
tiny forecastle the crew had turned in and were sleeping
too. Michael, under his awning on the after-deck, had been
fast asleep since eight o'clock. He was sleeping better
now than he'd slept for months, and he looked better. He
was brown as a coffee berry and his eyes, out here on
the sea he loved, had lost a lot of their brooding unhappi-
ness. Hearing him laugh sometimes, listening to his eager
little voice, one found it hard to believe that under the
straight, neatly-stretched sheets of his bed, his legs lay
useless and inert, and that they would lie thus for the
rest of his life. There were bad days, of course, even now.
Days when nothing could thrust from his mind the re-
membrance of his trapped helplessness, but they were
fewer and farther between than they'd been in Polpendor.
And when they came they were sooner over than they'd
been before. One had only to look at him even cursorily to
know that, despite his useless and motionless legs, he was
far and away better and stronger in himself than he'd been
since his accident, and seeing him in bed, his little brown
face against the white sheets, one couldn't believe-one
couldn't realize that save for one of the miracles that
happened so often in books but so rarely in real life, he
would have to lie there, still and quite helpless, for the rest
of his life. It wasn't to be wondered at, Kay thought, sit-
ting there in the darkness and remembering the things
Steele had told her, that to his father Michael meant so
much or that there were, etched into his face, lines far
too deep to belong, by rights, to a man of his age. She
heard his voice again, level and oddly expressionless, the
voice of a man who has schooled himself rigidly to keep a
watch on his tongue and on his words. "I hoped he would,
because I wanted you to know. As a matter of fact I asked
178
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
him to tell you. I thought-taking everything into con-

Kay said, because, faced with the tragedy of the man's

He could put any sort of wild construction on a speech

"Now, I've spoilt it all. I say things without thinking, and
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS 179
if once he imagines I'm getting sentimental about him or

But he had heard. He didn't move in his chair or look
at her. Neither his hands resting on his arms across his
chest moved, nor did his eyes shift for a moment from the
yacht's rail, but his voice when he answered her held a
note she'd never heard in it before. That sent a little shiver

her spine, and sent swinging wide open a door that, be-
wilderingly and startlingly let in on life a flood of sun-
shine she'd never until that moment known existed in the
world at all.
"Exactly," Francis Cresswell said quietly, "it's because
things are as they are that I wanted you to know how we
stood, Kay. That's all, my dear."
It was after one o'clock when they finally got up and
went below to their respective cabins, but to neither of
them did it seem strange that following on that quiet
"That's all, my dear," they should sit for more than an
hour and a half in a perfect unbroken silence.
But at the end of that hour and a half Francis Cress-
180
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
well got up, stretched long arms over his head and avoid-
ing her eyes, bade her good-night. Levelly, unemotionally,
as though, between them, the darkness still lay; as though
to neither of them had come that sudden blinding dazzle
of sunshine.
"Bedtime," he said. "It's gone one o'clock. 'Night,
Kay."
"'Night, Francis," Kay said.
Her voice was as level, as quiet, as expressionless as his
own, so that no one could possibly have guessed that, mak-
ing her way to her own small cabin, her feet, as she
walked, were treading on air, or that her heart was sing-
ing a Te Deum in gratitude for something to which it
seemed utter folly to give so much as a name.
And then, a week later, the miracle happened.
XVIII
CRESSWELL and Kay and Steele were finishing
breakfast when Mrs. Maloney came into the saloon. She
looked startled, and when she spoke her words tripped
over each other.
"Oh, Doctor, will ye come-Mr. Cresswell, 'tis some-

Steele was beside her in a flash, a hand on her arm.

reassuring. "Come on, Mrs. Maloney, get a hold on your-
self. What's happened?"

Glory be to God, Doctor, he can move his toe."

XIX
HAVING tea in Kay's tiny flat one afternoon at the
end of October, Tony said crossly:
"I can't think what the devil's come over you. You're
out of a job; you're in the black books of every manager
in London; you're known from here to Jericho as the
star who walked out without a single reasonable or log-
ical explanation; you've jeopardized every atom of influ-
ence you ever had; the chances are you'll starve before
you'll pick up another job of any kind, and you aren't
even depressed. You're smug. You're fat and well-look-
ing. You look like the cat who's eaten the canary and got
away with it, and you've no business to. You should be
eating humble pie unostentatiously and without making
any noise in the mastication of it either. You should be
making goo-goo eyes at even obscure managers; you
should be hanging round the necks of inferior and sec-
ond-rate playwrights and spreading the bread of fools
like Pam Dawson and Cora Marsland with thick dollops
of the jam of admiration and servility. And you're not.
You're doing nothing. You're sitting here and expecting
jobs to fall in your lap and they won't. What's come over
you? What's got into you? In the language of classic
America, what's eating you? Put into words of one syl-
lable so that you can't fail to understand, what the hell's
got you, Kay?"
"Nothing," Kay said. She took his cup, refilled it from
190
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS 191
the percolator bubbling on the hearth, gave it back to
him and smiled at him. "I shall get a job sooner or later.
I'm not worrying, Tony."
"But you ought to be worrying," Tony said. He put
down his cup, crammed half a sandwich into his mouth
and began to walk up and down the room with long irri-
table strides. "You ought," he said, muttering indis-
tinctly through what was left of the sandwich, "to be
out of your mind with anxiety. You're twenty-nine”
"Thirty in April," Kay said placidly. "I've always had
a passion for accuracy in details, darling."
"Thirty in April then," Tony said and crammed an-
other sandwich into his mouth, "and you've been back in
London-those sandwiches are good, Kay, mind if I go
on and finish 'em?-six weeks. Six weeks and what's hap-
pened? Where've you been? Who's seen you? Who's
asked you to their parties? Who's given you plays to
read? Nobody. Not a single damned soul, and the way
you're going on nobody will. Don't you want to get back,
Kay?"
"Yes," Kay said. She got up and went over to the
window. The flat was tiny and obscure and far too ex-

and a fancy; who had lost all her glamour, all her gaiety,

IT was Richards' suggestion made at the end of another
three months that Cresswell should take Michael for a
prolonged sea voyage.
"He's doing splendidly. He can walk and after a time
he'll be able to run, but for a bit he's got to go slowly.

I'm glad to say have been able to set him on his feet
physically. If you'll take my advice, Cresswell, you'll get
the boy a tutor who'll carry on the educational work
you've started and get him ready for school, and you'll
take him for a trip round the world. Give him another
year and I don't mind betting that he'll be as fit as a
fiddle. I've no doubt whatever but that even staying at

I'd take him away for a year. I'm inordinately pleased
with results as they are, but I'd give a good deal to see
what a year's sea trip would do for him, Cresswell, my
dear chap."

Against the creamy walls of the set Kay was looking
her best. She knew how to carry herself too. How to
make the most of every movement. When to stand utterly
still and motionless, when to augment her voice with her
hands. The part was only an indifferent one; the play
itself was indifferent for that matter, but the public ap-
parently liked it, asking of it nothing more than that it
should, for a couple of hours, take them out of themselves
and keep them amused. It was light, flippant, verging on
the risqué, its bowing acquaintance with actual vulgarity
only pushed into the background by the clever smartness
of its dialogue, and London loved it. It had been running

now for four months; would probably run on another

He hadn't meant to come to the show. He'd arranged
to meet Kay later and he'd meant to dine alone in Town
and then simply call for her and take her home, but at

the joy of watching her, bored him to tears from start

She'd be surprised of course, and probably pleased. He
hoped she'd be pleased, that her pleasure and her happi-

that Life, taking it by and large, had been almost intol-

the principals in them intended them to be. Servants talked

At the door that led to the stage door, the doorkeeper

"Miss Douglas'll be right along, sir. It don't take her

In the little narrow cheerless corridor he'd time to notice
quite a few things. Stage doors of course were no novelty.
He'd known those in Mary's time. Too well by far, but
he was surprised to realize how actively, how intensely he
disliked them and the atmosphere that was so indissolubly
part of them. A girl and boy, pushing past him, went out
into the quiet of the little side street outside the door. It
was so quiet one could scarcely realize that, fifty yards
away, was the crowded, swirling hum of Shaftesbury
Avenue and Piccadilly. A woman with tired haggard eyes
said: "Any letters for me, Hopkins?" with a note in her
voice that presupposed a negative answer, went past him
looking neither right nor to left and out through the
door. Stella McLean and Bill Conkling came out to-
gether, laughing, arm in arm. They would have supper
together somewhere and afterwards he'd see her home.
In a bus as far as it went, and then walking the rest of
the way down mediocre little streets that even still held the
unforgettable, unmistakable odor of boiled cabbage and
onions and fried fish. They were known to be in love
with each other, but with sense enough to see the folly
of getting married and adding to the worries of an over-
crowded profession, shortage of jobs, and living expenses
that soared out of all proportion to salaries.
Kay came out just behind them. It had turned chilly
and raw and over a summer frock she'd flung a coat of

pulled down over her hair suited her, made her look so

It was broad daylight with the sun streaming in
through uncurtained windows before she slept. Francis
had left just after one. He'd taken her hands in both his,
holding them closely and tightly but he hadn't kissed her.
He hadn't even tried to kiss her and their leave-taking
had been utterly devoid of emotion of any kind. It seemed
to Kay that their souls and bodies had been so drained
of emotion in the years gone by as to leave them inca-
pable of anything but the commonplace.
"It's been a wonderful time," Kay had said, and
Cresswell had nodded. Somberly.
"Although you prefer to see it end," he'd said. "You
women are queer, you know, Kay. I'd have staked my
216
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
soul that you'd have been willing to come with me, and
instead" There he'd dropped her hands, picked up his
hat and coat and stick and gone to the door. "Good-bye,
my dear, and thank you. I've a great deal for which to be
grateful to you in one way and another. Perhaps even
more than you know. But good-by, Kay."
After he'd gone Kay had stood quite still where he'd
left her on the hearthrug. She could hear his footsteps on
the stairs, the closing of the front door, his steps again
on the pavement outside. With an odd impersonal sense
of detachment she was conscious that the gas fire seemed
to sizzle and hiss more noisily than was its usual habit;
that the tap in the kitchenette was dripping into a sauce-
pan she'd left full of water in the sink. It played almost a
little tune as it dripped-phut-phut-phut-tut-tut-phut-
phut-ut-phut-and she found herself thinking that she
must remember to speak of it next day to the landlord.
There had been a notice in the paper only that morning
warning people against careless waste of water. She went
out into the kitchenette and tried to stop it, but she only
succeeded in changing the time a little and it was still
dripping when she turned off the fire and in the tiny bed-
room next door began to get ready for bed. But after all
it didn't matter much really. Nothing really mattered very
much it seemed, now that Francis was gone. Now that
she'd actually sent him away. From her bed she could see
through the uncurtained window across the tops of the
houses on the opposite side of the road. Between the gables
of No. 18 and No. 20 a star winked at her out of a sky
that wasn't black, but was instead just a dark depressing
gray. Winking at her through the window the star seemed
to mock her. To laugh at her for her absurd Quixotry,
to jeer at her for her folly. It almost put into words the
thoughts that were chasing each other through her brain.
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
217
"What a fool you've been. D'you realize that he's gone
and that you've sent him away simply because you're a
fool with ideals as absurd as his own? D'you know you'll
never see him again and that you've failed him? He loved
you and wanted you and you've failed him. You've sent
him away-alone. What d'you suppose he's thinking of
you tonight-now that you've sent him away?"
But to that at least Kay had an answer. It was the
only bright spot in the awful loneliness that stretched out
ahead of her; that made a future without Francis even
bearable; that brought to her weary soul even a grain of
comfort. Francis had asked her to go away with him. He'd
even tried to persuade her, but deep down in his heart
he'd been glad when she'd refused. He hadn't said so, but
she'd sensed it quite clearly. He hadn't changed his mind
at all really. He'd only pushed ideals into the background
of their existence. The ideals that had governed his life
weren't dead. Even his love for her hadn't been able to
kill them, so that he'd simply pushed them into the back-
ground hoping against a hope he knew to be vain that if
he pushed them sufficiently far they'd never have the cour-
age or the strength to come back again in the open. But
they weren't dead. In Francis they never would be dead.
They were a part of him as definitely as were his hands
and his feet and his ability to perform marvelous things
on the organ. While Francis lived, his ideals would live
with him despite his resolve to ignore their existence.
They'd sit behind him, whispering in his ear, nagging at
him, jibing at him, so that life would become unbearable
and intolerable, and there would be neither peace nor rest
nor even a modicum of happiness for him or for any one
else, and he'd known it.
Lying there alone, watching the star fade with the pass-
ing of night; watching the dark gray curtains of the sky
218
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
part to make room for the pink-tipped fingers of another
day, Kay faced the truth without flinching. Francis had
asked her to go; he'd even urged her to reconsider her
refusal, but there'd been no mistaking the relief in his
eyes when she'd stuck quite unshakably to her final de-
cision. He loved her and wanted her, that she knew. Knew,
too, that for a long time the fulfillment of the love they'd
borne each other so long would have kept the ideals so far
in the background that they would have been unable to
hurt; that passion and satisfaction of passion would have
trampled on remorse, so that for a time it wouldn't have
had the power even to raise its ugly head. But passion
wouldn't last for ever. There would come a time when it
would stand aside and make room once again for the
ideals it had pushed into the background, for the remorse
on which it had so ruthlessly and effectively trampled,
and when life would become unbearable. When neither
love nor even friendship could stand the strain of the nag-
ging of a conscience one had tried to kill and couldn't.
Life alone would be bad. There would be times when it
would be almost intolerable. But even life alone would be
better than standing by, helpless, while the man one loved
as one had loved nothing and no one in the world, fought
a losing battle with the ideals he couldn't kill.

‘NOW listen, darling," Gordon Richways said with a

Douglas a chance now"
Brian Onslow, passing the table, caught the name and
stopped. "Were you talking of Kay Douglas? Where is
she anyway? It's donkey's years since I've even heard of
her. D'you know where she is, Seabright?"
"Yes," Tony said. "I've known all along. It's only fools
who don't know."
Richways said scowling:
"The Dawson girl's chucked her part," and Onslow
laughed.
"You never expected she'd make a go of it, even if she
kept it, did you?" he said. "Listen here, Richways. The
sooner you see things in their proper perspective the better
it'll be for you. Pam Dawson's the loveliest thing on the
stage and when you've said that you've said all there is to
say. And it's time you knew it. She's scraped through
things because of her looks and because there isn't a
woman on the job who can get as much out of clothes as
she can get. She's a genius that way, and then you've said
it. Apart from her body and her voice-and I grant you
she's got both those there isn't a thing to her. Not one
damn thing."
Richways said sulkily:
"Arguing over the brains Pam Dawson hasn't got won't
help. Unless I delay production-which is a thing I've
never done-I've got five days to fill the part. Five days,
and it's the biggest part going"
"Give it to Kay," Tony said, sticking to his guns. "Lis-
ten, Richways, I'll guarantee"
Richways snapped at him:
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS 225
"Don't talk drivel. An archangel couldn't guarantee the
acts of any woman with any degree of certainty. They're
unreliable and untrustworthy; they're on the job one min-
ute and off at a tangent the next. They don't know the
meaning of stability or reason or ordinary horse-sense.
They're round your neck at lunch and waiting for you
with a knife round a dark corner before tea. Women-
my God, if women didn't have to be taken into considera-
tion"
Onslow said slowly:
"I'm inclined to believe in Tony's suggestion just the
same, Richways. Kay's a grand actress and she's a worker.
If she takes on the job"
"She'll walk out of it when it pleases her," Richways
snarled. "And when she walked out on me last time I
swore I'd never have her in a show of mine again. I don't
talk for the sake of talking either, and when I say a thing
I mean it"
"You'll still be meaning what you said when you have
to go round crawling to Pam Dawson, won't you?" Tony
said relentlessly. "You'll love that, won't you, Richways?
Just think how Pam'll love it too. She'll have you just
where she wants you, once you've eaten humble-pie and
gone round apologizing to her and asking her to come
back and take up the part again. And you can bet your
life she'll make the best of her chance. If I were in your
shoes I'd a damn sight sooner go back on my word to
myself than put myself in a position where Pam Dawson
could ride me as she'll ride you once she gets her hands
on the reins. It's your funeral, of course, but I'd have to
be in a far tighter corner than you're in before I'd give a
woman like Pam Dawson the chance of riding rough-shod
over me, Richways."
In the end, of course, he won his point.
226
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
Richways said:
"If I give her the job and she walks out on me I'll have
her name blackballed in every agent's office in the country.
I'll make it so hot for her that she'll never get so much
as a walk on anywhere in the country”
"She'll never have to look for a job again if you give
her this one," Tony said jubilantly. He pushed his chair
back and got up. "I'll have her round at your place in a
couple of hours, Richways, and I'll eat my hat if you
ever regret it."

THE box office manager repeated for the two hundredth
time the phrase he had used continuously since four-thirty
the previous day.
...
...
"Sorry, sir, not a thing left. No, sir, nothing any-
where. Sold out since last night. Next week, if that'll
be any use to you...."
At his elbow a harassed assistant said the same thing
into a telephone receiver. Like his chief, he had been
repeating it steadily for the best part of ten hours, so that
his voice had ceased to hold the slightest resemblance to
a human organ and was instead simply a part of the in-
strument that rang continuously and infuriatingly in his
ear. Curt, metallic, impersonal.
"Sorry, Madam.... No, nothing...absolutely noth-
ing... entirely sold out.... Perhaps next week... or the
week after...." In the foyer, greeting and being greeted,
Gordon Richways could be forgiven the little thrill of
smug satisfaction that ran up his spine as the crowd
surged past him. It was good to be responsible for a show
like this, good to know that fifteen years' grueling at his
job had produced a result of this kind; had made a Rich-
ways first night not merely an event of more than usual
importance in the theatrical world but, at the same time,
an event of equally noticeable importance in the London
social world as well. Every one who was any one came to a
Richways first night. Garbed as elaborately as for a formal

It's seven years since Kay played Brenda. They ate her

His thoughts switched to Kay as he had seen her an

SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS 229
prestige and his position as the greatest producer in Lon-

XXIII
THE BETTER THING had run for ten months and
had been replaced by Brian Onslow's Silver Sixpence when
Richard Veryan came down to spend Sunday at the tiny
Hertfordshire cottage he'd found for her, and asked her
to marry him.
They'd played golf all morning and then, with lunch
over, they'd gone together out into the garden. Old Mattie
had put out long steamer chairs and they'd sat out there,
half-asleep in the hot July sunshine, Kay's knitting neg-
lected on her lap, the paper Richard had brought out to
read lying unheeded on the grass beside him, and with
between them the table on which Mattie had put all the
things they might be most likely to want. A couple of
uncut novels, a jug of lemon squash, a bowl of fruit and
an unopened box of chocolates. Tony had been down to
lunch and gone again.
He'd said:
"You're getting smug, Kay. And altogether too self-
sufficient. Which is a pity, because women shouldn't be
self-sufficient. It detracts from their charm. If, for ex-
ample, instead of sitting there, entirely independent and
cocksure of yourself, you were to say, 'Having you here
with me, Tony, is such a relief. I'm so afraid of being
alone. With you here I always feel that nothing could ever
really hurt me,' I should immediately cease to feel an en-
cumbrance and a beneficiary under your charity and feel
237
238
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
instead intensely masculine and protective, which sensation
would add at least three inches to my mental and moral
stature and make me feel a damn fine fellow. You haven't
much tact you know, Kay."
"I've as much tact as you have sense, darling," Kay
said equably. "And in any case I'm not even alone. I
hardly ever am alone for that matter, because there's al-
ways Mattie and almost always there's Richard into the
bargain. At all events down here. You've got to admit that
I couldn't truthfully admit loneliness, Tony, especially
over Sundays."

I regret to say." He took both her hands in his and stood

240
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
he's wrong. I've made myself seem self-sufficient because

Richard said, interrupting her:

SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS

things for that week, Richard, I'll be quite ready to come

THEY lunched together at the Tramps' Club on the

little pink and blue and purple blossoms that reminded

SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
tally, but one knew the end would be the same. It would,

XXV
RICHARDVERYAN saw them leaving the stage
door just as he drove up. They walked hand in hand, and
it was as clear to him as it must have been to any casual
onlooker that, apart from each other, no one else existed
in the world at all. He stood watching them for a mo-
ment, then gave the doorkeeper half a crown and went
through the swinging door that led into the passage and
along it, his feet echoing eerily in the silence, to Kay's
dressing-room.
"Miss Douglas has gone, hasn't she, Mattie?" he said,
and Mattie beamed at him.
"Ay, bless her heart, she's gone," she told him. "Talk
of Romance-well, I'll tell you. Come back straight as it
might be from the grave, just when she least expected him
and all. Ay, she's gone right enough. To get married, I
guess, judgin' by the looks of things, but she left a note
fer you, yer Lordship. Scribbled it so quick it'll be a
wonder if you'll ever be able to read it, but it's here
whether you can read it or not, and I'm sure I'm
much obliged to you, sir."
The note was quite brief. Written in pencil on the back
of a torn envelope and obviously in a hurry.
"Oh, Richard, forgive me, but Francis has come back.
He is free and we're to be married at once. You wouldn't,
would you, want to keep me in the rain with the sun
255
256
SUNLIGHT ON THE HILLS
86
shining so gloriously where I can get into it? I shall want
you always as my dearest and best friend, but Francis is
Francis, and I must go with him. Forgive me, Richard,
and if you can, be kind to me and wish me luck.
"KAY."
He stood there for a moment with the envelope in his
hands, unconscious of old Mattie's watching eye, and with
before him the vision of an unseen hand that drew in-
flexibly, inevitably, a black curtain between himself and
all the things that had, for one whole glorious anticipatory
fortnight, made life so wonderful. Then he tore the en-
velope across and across, dropped the bits into the waste-
paper basket that stood beside the dressing-table, and
picked up the hat and stick he had laid down in order to
read it.
"It's all right, Mattie," he said. "When you see Miss
Douglas tell her that, will you? Tell her she deserves all
the sun she can get, and that I'm glad." He saw her
puzzled, questioning eyes, and pulled himself up short.
"Give her my love, and tell her it's quite all right and-
good-night, Mattie."
